Generalized affine buildings for semisimple algebraic groups over real closed fields Thanks: Address : Department of Mathematics, Heidelberg University, Germany Thanks: E-mail : rappenzeller@mathi.uni-heidelberg.de
Raphael Appenzeller

Abstract
. We use real algebraic geometry to construct an affine Λ \Lambda -building ℬ \mathcal{B} associated to the 𝔽 \mathbb{F} -points of a semisimple algebraic group, where 𝔽 \mathbb{F} is a valued real closed field. We characterize the spherical building at infinity and the local building at a base point. We compute stabilizers of various subsets of ℬ \mathcal{B} and obtain group decompositions.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification

51E24, 20G25, 14P10, 12J25

1.
Introduction

Buildings were introduced by Jacques Tits as an analogue of Riemannian symmetric spaces for algebraic groups. In their seminal work
[
BT72
,
BT84a
]
, Bruhat and Tits associate (not necessarily discrete) affine buildings to reductive groups over fields with real valuations. Other constructions of affine buildings have since been found in terms of lattices, seminorms
[
BT84b
,
BT87
,
Par00
]
and asymptotic cones
[
KL97
]
. In this work, we study symmetric spaces from a real algebraic point of view to construct non-discrete affine buildings associated to algebraic groups over real closed valued fields following ideas from
[
Bru88b
,
KT02
]
. We actually work in the more general framework of affine
\Lambda
-buildings, which were introduced by
[
Ben94
]
to allow for valuations to arbitrary ordered abelian groups
\Lambda
. Recently, the Bruhat-Tits construction has also been generalized to this setting by
[
HIL23
]
.

Riemannian symmetric spaces of non-compact type are determined by their symmetry groups which are Lie groups that are (essentially) the
\mathbb{R}
-points
G_{\mathbb{R}}
of semisimple algebraic groups
G<\operatorname{SL}_{n}
. In this work, we replace the reals
\mathbb{R}
by a real closed field
\mathbb{F}
with a valuation to some ordered abelian group
\Lambda
. In analogy to the construction of symmetric spaces from
G_{\mathbb{R}}
, we use
G_{\mathbb{F}}
to define a
\Lambda
-metric space
\mathcal{B}
on which
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts by isometries. Under a technical assumption, we show that
\mathcal{B}
admits the structure of an affine
\Lambda
-building.

Theorem
5.1
.

If the root system
\Sigma
of the Lie group
G_{\mathbb{R}}
is reduced (
\alpha\in\Sigma
implies
2\alpha\notin\Sigma
), then
\mathcal{B}
is an affine
\Lambda
-building of type
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(\Sigma^{\vee},\Lambda,\Lambda^{\operatorname{rk}(G)})
.

In Section
6
, we investigate the building at infinity
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
and the local building
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
at a base point
o\in\mathcal{B}
, as introduced in
[
Ben94
]
and
[
Sch09
]
.

Theorem
6.4
.

The group
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts strongly transitively on
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
, which can therefore be identified with the spherical building associated to
G_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Theorem
6.1
.

Let
k
be the residue field of the valued field
\mathbb{F}
and
G_{k}
the
k
-extension of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
. The group
G_{k}
acts strongly transitively on
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
, which can therefore be identified with the spherical building associated to
G_{k}
.

Real closed fields
\mathbb{F}
are ordered fields that have the same first-order logic theory as the reals. In
[
App25
]
, the author used a transfer principle from model theory to recover many results about Lie groups in the setting of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
; these include group decompositions, subgroup structure and Kostant convexity. The paper
[
App25
]
may be considered as a prerequisite to this paper, as all the results there are used in this paper.

1.1.
The construction of
\mathcal{B}

Since
\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
acts transitively on

P_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneq\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\colon x=x{{}^{\mathsf{T}}\!},\,\det(x)=1,\,x\text{ is positive definite }\right\}

(by
g.x\coloneq gxg{{}^{\mathsf{T}}\!}
) and the stabilizer of
\operatorname{Id}\in P_{\mathbb{R}}
is
\operatorname{SO}(n)
,
P_{\mathbb{R}}
is a semialgebraic (defined by equalities and inequalities of polynomials) model for the symmetric space
\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})/\operatorname{SO}(n,\mathbb{R})
.
Every symmetric space of non-compact type can be realized as a totally geodesic submanifold of
P_{\mathbb{R}}
. In fact, for every symmetric space
M
of non-compact type there exists a semisimple self-adjoint (
\forall g\in G_{\mathbb{R}},g{{}^{\mathsf{T}}\!}\in G_{\mathbb{R}}
) algebraic subgroup
G_{\mathbb{R}}<\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
such that the orbit
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneq G_{\mathbb{R}}.\operatorname{Id}\subseteq P_{\mathbb{R}}
is a semialgebraic model for
M

[
Ebe96
, Theorem 2.6.5]
. The symmetric space
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
comes equipped with a
G_{\mathbb{R}}
-invariant Riemannian distance: the maximal flats are isometric to a Euclidean vector space and for any two points there is a maximal flat containing the two, so the distance is given as the norm of the difference of the two corresponding points in the vector space.

The construction of the building
\mathcal{B}
mimics the above description closely, the main difference being, that we now work over a valued real closed field
\mathbb{F}
, see Sections
3
and
4
for definitions. In real algebraic geometry, semialgebraic sets are considered over general real closed fields. We call the
\mathbb{F}
-extension
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
the
non-standard symmetric space
. The
\mathbb{F}
-extension
G_{\mathbb{F}}
of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
with stabilizer

K_{\mathbb{F}}\coloneq G_{\mathbb{F}}\cap\operatorname{SO}(n,\mathbb{F})=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\operatorname{Id}).

Let
A_{\mathbb{F}}<G_{\mathbb{F}}
be the
\mathbb{F}
-extension of the semialgebraic connected component of a maximal self-adjoint
\mathbb{F}
-split torus of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
containing the identity
[
App25
, Theorem
]
. For any two points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
, let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
y=g.x
. The Cartan decomposition
G_{\mathbb{F}}=K_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}K_{\mathbb{F}}

[
App25
, Theorem
]
can then be used to obtain
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g=kak^{\prime}
for some
k
,
k^{\prime}\in K_{\mathbb{F}}
. In fact,
a
can be taken in lie in the non-standard fundamental Weyl cone

A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}\coloneq\left\{a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\chi_{\alpha}(a)\geq 1\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}\right\},

where
\chi_{\alpha}
are algebraic characters associated to the root system
\Sigma
of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
. We prove that this defines a map.

Lemma
4.2
.

The Cartan-projection
\delta_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\to A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+},\,(x,y)\mapsto a

is well-defined and invariant under the action of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
.

We then define the semialgebraic multiplicative norm

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{F}}\colon A_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{F}_{\geq 1},\ a\mapsto\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\max\left\{\chi_{\alpha}(a),\chi_{\alpha}(a)^{-1}\right\}.

Over the reals,
d\coloneq\log\circ N_{\mathbb{R}}\circ\delta_{\mathbb{R}}
defines a Finsler-metric on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
. However, the logarithm may not be defined on
\mathbb{F}
, since it is not a semialgebraic function, but we can replace
\log
by a order compatible valuation
v\colon\mathbb{F}_{\neq 0}\to\Lambda
, where
\Lambda
is an ordered abelian group, to obtain a symmetric non-negative function

\displaystyle d\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\to\Lambda,\ (x,y)\mapsto(-v)\left(N_{\mathbb{F}}\left(\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(x,y)\right)\right).

The function
d
is not quite a
\Lambda
-metric, since there are points
x\neq y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
with
d(x,y)=0
, but we have the following.

Theorem
4.5
.

The function
d\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\to\Lambda
is a pseudo-distance.

The proof of Theorem
4.5
relies on the Iwasawa retraction whose properties follow from Kostant convexity, see
[
App25
, Theorems
and
]
.

Theorem
4.4
.

The Iwasawa retraction
\rho\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\to A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}
is a
d
-diminishing retraction to
A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}
.

The main object of our investigation is defined as the quotient
\mathcal{B}\coloneq\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}/\!\!\sim
, where
x\sim y
whenever
d(x,y)=0\in\Lambda
. By Theorem
4.5
,
\mathcal{B}
is a
\Lambda
-metric space. We note that
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts transitively by isometries on
\mathcal{B}
. Let
o\coloneq[\operatorname{Id}]\in\mathcal{B}
. In Section
4.4
we verify that
\mathbb{A}\coloneq A_{\mathbb{F}}.o\subseteq\mathcal{B}
is an apartment in the sense of affine
\Lambda
-buildings. The inclusion
f_{0}\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathcal{B}
can be used to define an atlas
\mathscr{F}\coloneq\left\{g.f_{0}\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathcal{B}\colon g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}\right\}
. The main result, Theorem
5.1
, states that when
\Sigma
is reduced, the
\Lambda
-metric space
\mathcal{B}
together with the atlas
\mathscr{F}
is an affine
\Lambda
-building.

The definition of
\mathcal{B}
required relatively few results about the structure of algebraic groups compared with the definition of the building in Bruhat-Tits
[
BT72
]
. However, the proof of our main result that
\mathcal{B}
is an affine
\Lambda
-building does rely on a deep understanding of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
and the action of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
on
\mathcal{B}
. Most of the results about
G_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
can be deduced from known results for Lie groups using the transfer principle in real algebraic geometry. However, since the valuation
v
is not semialgebraic, statements about
\mathcal{B}
typically have to be proven more directly. We highlight some of these results of independent interest.

1.2.
The action of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
on
\mathcal{B}

A central theme in the proof of Theorem
5.1
is to study the pointwise stabilizers of subsets of the apartment
\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. Recall from
[
App25
, Section
]
that an order on the root system
\Sigma
allows us to define
U_{\mathbb{F}}
as the exponential of the sum of root spaces corresponding to positive roots. Let
N_{\mathbb{F}}
and
M_{\mathbb{F}}
be the
\mathbb{F}
-extensions of the semialgebraic groups
N_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneq\operatorname{Nor}_{K_{\mathbb{R}}}(A_{\mathbb{R}})
and
M_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneq\operatorname{Cen}_{K_{\mathbb{R}}}(A_{\mathbb{R}})
. Let
O\coloneq\left\{a\in\mathbb{F}\colon(-v)(a)\leq 0\right\}
be the valuation ring associated to the valuation
v
and let
E_{\mathbb{F}}(O)\coloneq E_{\mathbb{F}}\cap\operatorname{SL}(n,O)
for any semialgebraic subset
E_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq G_{\mathbb{F}}
.

The stabilizer of
o\in\mathcal{B}
was calculated by
[
Tho02
]
for the special case when
\mathbb{F}
is a Robinson field. For general fields, it has been suggested by
[
KT02
]
and independently proven by
[
BIPP25
]
.

Theorem
4.19
.

The stabilizer of a base point
o\in\mathcal{B}
in
G_{\mathbb{F}}
is
G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
.

As a consequence of the Iwasawa retraction Theorem
4.4
, we give an Iwasawa decomposition of the stabilizer of
o
.

Corollary
4.20
.

There is an Iwasawa decomposition
G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)=U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)K_{\mathbb{F}}
, meaning that for every
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
there are unique
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
,
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
and
k\in K_{\mathbb{F}}=K_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
with
g=uak
.

In Section
5
we implement some ideas from
[
BT72
]
in the semialgebraic setting. For
\alpha\in\Sigma
, the root groups are
(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\coloneq\exp((\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}})
,
and we introduce explicit root group valuations
\varphi_{\alpha}\colon(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\to\Lambda\cup\{-\infty\}
in Subsection
5.2
. Taking a closer look at the unipotent group, we notice that if
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}
sends some point of the apartment to some other point of the apartment, then the two points have to be the same.

Proposition
5.7
.

For all
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}
and
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
,
ua.o\in\mathbb{A}\iff ua.o=a.o.

In fact, the fixed point set of elements in
(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
is a half-apartment.

Proposition
5.8
.

Let
\alpha\in\Sigma
. For
u\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
we have

\left\{p\in\mathbb{A}\colon u.p\in\mathbb{A}\right\}=\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\leq(-v)\left(\chi_{\alpha}\left(a\right)\right)\right\}

and therefore this set is a half-apartment when
u\neq\operatorname{Id}
.

For
U_{\mathbb{F}}
, this can be upgraded to finite intersections of half-apartments.

Proposition
5.10
.

For
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}
there are
k_{\alpha}\in\Lambda\cup\{-\infty\}
for
\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}
such that

\{p\in\mathbb{A}\colon u.p\in\mathbb{A}\}=\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon k_{\alpha}\leq(-v)\left(\chi_{\alpha}\left(a\right)\right)\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}\right\}

and therefore the set of fixed points is a finite intersection of half-apartments. If
u
fixes all of
\mathbb{A}
, then
u=\operatorname{Id}
.

We proceed to describe the pointwise stabilizers of the whole apartment
\mathbb{A}
, the half-apartments

H_{\alpha}^{+}\coloneq\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))\geq 0\right\}

and the fundamental Weyl chamber

C_{0}\coloneq\bigcap_{\alpha>0}H_{\alpha}^{+}.

Theorem
5.19
.

The pointwise stabilizer of
\mathbb{A}
in
G_{\mathbb{F}}
is
A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Theorem
5.20
.

Let
\alpha\in\Sigma
. The pointwise stabilizer of
H_{\alpha}^{+}
is
(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(O)A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Theorem
5.17
.

The pointwise stabilizer of
C_{0}
in
G_{\mathbb{F}}
is

U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}.

In Subsection
5.6
we take a closer look at the rank one subgroup
(L_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}<G_{\mathbb{F}}
introduced in
[
App25
, Section
]
. We now restrict to groups
G_{\mathbb{F}}
with reduced root systems, to be able to use the Jacobson-Morozov Lemma to find elements
m(u)\in N_{\mathbb{F}}
representing a reflection along some affine hyperplane
M_{\alpha,\ell}=\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))=\ell\right\}
for
\alpha\in\Sigma
and
\ell\in\Lambda
. A careful analysis of the rank one subgroup
L=\left\langle(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}},(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\right\rangle<(L_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
results in the following decomposition of its stabilizer.

Proposition
5.39
.

Assume
\Sigma
is reduced. Let
\alpha\in\Sigma
,
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
a non-empty finite subset. The pointwise stabilizers

\displaystyle L_{\Omega}

\displaystyle\coloneq L\cap\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle U_{\alpha,\Omega}

\displaystyle\coloneq(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cap\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle U_{-\alpha,\Omega}

\displaystyle\coloneq(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cap\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega),

\displaystyle N_{\Omega}

\displaystyle\coloneq\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cap\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)

satisfy
L_{\Omega}=\left\langle U_{\alpha,\Omega},U_{-\alpha,\Omega},(N_{\Omega}\cap L)\right\rangle=U_{\alpha,\Omega}U_{-\alpha,\Omega}(N_{\Omega}\cap L)
.

In Subsection
5.7
we upgrade the rank one result to
G_{\mathbb{F}}
. For
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
, let

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left\langle N_{\Omega},U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma\right\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\Omega}^{+}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left\langle U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle U_{\Omega}^{-}

\displaystyle\coloneq\left\langle U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma_{<0}\right\rangle.

Theorem
5.49
.

Assume
\Sigma
is reduced. The pointwise stabilizer of any subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
satisfies

\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)=\hat{P}_{\Omega}=U_{\Omega}^{+}U_{\Omega}^{-}N_{\Omega}.

The proof of Theorem
5.49
is tightly intertwined with the proof of axiom (A2) of affine buildings. It first relies on the following
mixed Iwasawa
decomposition for
G_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Theorem
5.42
.

Assume
\Sigma
is reduced. Then
G_{\mathbb{F}}=U_{\mathbb{F}}\cdot\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cdot G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
.

With the help of the mixed Iwasawa decomposition we obtain Theorem
5.49
in the case of finite subsets
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
. This statement for finite subsets is then enough to prove the second part of axiom (A2). The full Theorem
5.49
then follows from this part of (A2) and the rest of axiom (A2) follows from the full Theorem
5.49
. We also obtain a description of the (not necessarily pointwise) stabilizer of
\mathbb{A}
.

Proposition
5.52
.

The stabilizer of
\mathbb{A}
is
\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})=A_{\mathbb{F}}M_{\mathbb{F}}
.

1.3.
Related work

There are multiple connections between symmetric spaces and buildings. Mostow associated a building at infinity to symmetric spaces of non-compact type to prove rigidity results on lattices in higher rank Lie groups
[
Mos73
]
. Kleiner and Leeb showed that the asymptotic cone of a symmetric space of non-compact type is a non-discrete affine
\mathbb{R}
-building and used this result to show rigidity of quasi-isometries in symmetric spaces of non-compact type
[
KL97
]
. The asymptotic cone is a special case of the building
\mathcal{B}
, when the field
\mathbb{F}
is a Robinson field
[
BIPP25
, Theorem 1.11]
. Motivated to give a simpler proof of the rigidity of quasi-isometries, Kramer and Tent
[
KT02
]
suggested the construction of
\mathcal{B}
, which is carried out in detail in the present work.

A newer promising direction where
\mathcal{B}
plays an important role is the study of geometric structures on surfaces, and more generally the study of character varieties, for a survey see
[
Wie18
]
. Character varieties are spaces of (equivalence classes of) representations of discrete groups into Lie groups. Their properties can be studied using various compactifications. Thurston
[
Thu88
]
constructed a compactification of the space of discrete and faithful representations of a fundamental group of a surface of genus at least two into the isometry group of the hyperbolic plane. Thurston described the boundary points in terms of actions on certain
\mathbb{R}
-trees and used a fixed point theorem to classify elements of the mapping class group. In higher rank, similar compactifications have been developed, such as the marked length compactification
[
Bon88
,
Par12
]
. The real spectrum compactification
[
Bru88a
,
BIPP21
,
BIPP25
]
is a finer compactification with some good properties, for instance it preserves connected components. Similarly to Thurston’s description,
[
Bru88a
,
Bru88b
]
and
[
BIPP25
, Theorem 1.1]
show that the boundary points correspond to an action on
\mathcal{B}
, which is a
\Lambda
-tree in rank one, or more generally a
\Lambda
-building, as we show in Theorem
5.1
, see
[
Jae25
]
for more interpretations of boundary points. The real algebraic approach taken for the real spectrum compactification harmonizes closely with the construction of
\mathcal{B}
. This approach has also proved useful to study Hitchin components and projective structures
[
Fla22
,
FP25
]
. A unifying approach to geometric structures are
\Theta
-positive representations: it is now known that all
\Theta
-positive components consist of discrete and faithful representations
[
BGL+24
]
. We would like to point out that the technical condition on the root system in Theorem
5.1
is always satisfied in the case of
\Theta
-positive representations
[
GW25
]
. Theorems
6.1
and
6.4
about the global and local structure of
\mathcal{B}
are promising tools to understand degenerations of geometric structures. When
\Lambda<\mathbb{R}
it is known that
\mathcal{B}
is contained in some affine
\mathbb{R}
-building
[
SS12
]
which in turn is contained in a complete affine
\mathbb{R}
-building
[
Str11
, Lemma 4.4]
, which is a CAT(0) space. The completion
\overline{\mathcal{B}}
of
\mathcal{B}
(viewed as a convex subset of a complete CAT(0)-space) is then a CAT(0) space and fixed point theorems can be applied.

The construction of
\mathcal{B}
is also interesting from the point of view of the theory of affine
\Lambda
-buildings. In the beginning, the only known examples of affine
\Lambda
-buildings with
\Lambda\neq\mathbb{R}
were the ones of type
A_{n}
in Bennet’s paper
[
Ben94
]
, where he introduced the concept. In their paper on functoriality
[
SS12
]
, Schwer and Struyve showed how to construct new
\Lambda
-buildings from others. Recently, Hébert, Izquierdo and Loisel
[
HIL23
]
generalized the Bruhat-Tits construction to
\Lambda\neq\mathbb{R}
. Their construction works for split groups, or quasi-split groups over Henselian fields. The real closed fields we consider are not always Henselian and the groups we consider need to be semi-simple, but not necessarily quasi-split, so our construction
\mathcal{B}
gives new examples of affine
\Lambda
-buildings. While the definition of the Bruhat-Tits building
[
BT72
,
BT84a
]
and the generalization in
[
HIL23
]
requires quite a deep understanding of algebraic groups, the definition of
\mathcal{B}
is relatively elementary: it is a quotient of a non-standard symmetric space. However for the proof that
\mathcal{B}
is an affine
\Lambda
-building, we rely extensively on the theory of algebraic groups over real closed fields as developed in
[
App25
]
. To our knowledge, all examples of non-discrete affine buildings come from algebraic groups. It would be interesting to exhibit exotic non-discrete affine buildings, analogous to the discrete setting. Discrete affine buildings have been fully classified
[
Wei09
]
, a similar classification in the non-discrete case is open, though partial results in the case of
\mathbb{R}
-buildings can be found in
[
Tit86
]
.

1.4.
Acknowledgements

6
which is new) is the main second part of the author’s doctoral thesis
[
App24
]
, but Section
4.4
has been substantially reformulated. The first part of the thesis is contained in
[
App25
]
which at the time of writing is under review for publication. The author would like to thank Marc Burger for continued support and is grateful for useful discussions with Petra Schwer and Auguste Hébert, in particular for their insistence on reading
[
BT72
]
carefully. The author is thankful for valuable inputs by Linus Kramer, Gabriel Pallier, Luca de Rosa, Xenia Flamm, Victor Jaeck and Segev Gonen Cohen. The author greatly appreciated the detailed and careful comments by an anonymous reviewer.

2.
Affine
\Lambda
-buildings

2.1.
\Lambda
-metric spaces

An abelian group
(\Lambda,+)
with a total order such that
x,y\geq 0
implies
x+y\geq 0
for all
x,y\in\Lambda
is called an
ordered abelian group
. If an ordered abelian group
\Lambda
is isomorphic (as an ordered group) to a subgroup of
(\mathbb{R},+)
, it is called
Archimedean
. Hahn’s embedding theorem classifies all ordered abelian groups.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
Hah07
]
)
.

For every ordered abelian group
(\Lambda,+)
there is a ordered set
\Omega
such that
\Lambda<\mathbb{R}^{\Omega}
as an ordered subgroup, where

\mathbb{R}^{\Omega}=\{f\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\colon\operatorname{supp}(f)\text{ is contained in a well-ordered set }\}

is equipped with the lexicographical ordering.

Let
(\Lambda,+)
be a non-trivial ordered abelian group. In particular,
\Lambda
has no torsion.
We will use the following generalization of metric spaces.
If
X
is a set and
d\colon X\times X\to\Lambda
is a function, we call
(X,d)
a
\Lambda
-pseudometric space
if for all
x,y,z\in X

(1)

d(x,x)=0
,
d(x,y)\geq 0

(2)

d(x,y)=d(y,x)

(3)

d(x,y)\leq d(x,z)+d(z,y)
.

If in addition,
d(x,y)=0
implies
x=y
, then
(X,d)
is called a
\Lambda
-metric space
. The axioms are direct generalizations of the notions of (pseudo)metric spaces when
\Lambda=\mathbb{R}
. Any
\Lambda
-pseudometric space can be turned into a
\Lambda
-metric space by quotienting out the equivalence relation of having distance
0
.

Important examples of
\Lambda
-metric spaces are
\Lambda
-trees
[
Chi01
]
, which serve as rank one examples of affine
\Lambda
-buildings.

2.2.
Root systems

A detailed treatment of root systems can be found in
[
Bou08
]
. Let
V
be a finite dimensional Euclidean vector space with scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. For
\alpha\in V
, the reflection along the hyperplane

M_{\alpha}=\{\beta\in~V\colon\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=0\}

is given by

r_{\alpha}(\beta)=\beta-2\frac{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\alpha.

A pair
(\Phi,V)
where
\Phi\subseteq V
is called a
root system
if

(R1)

\Phi
is finite, symmetric (
\Phi=-\Phi
), spans
V
and does not contain
0
.

(R2)

For every
\alpha\in\Phi
the reflection
r_{\alpha}\colon V\to V
preserves
\Phi
.

When
V
can be determined from the context, the root system may be denoted by just
\Phi
. A root system is called
crystallographic
if it satisfies the integrality condition

(R3)

If
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
, then
2\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle/\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
.

For crystallographic root systems,
\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)
is a lattice in
V
. A root system is called
reduced
if

(R4)

For every
\alpha\in\Phi
,
\mathbb{R}\cdot\alpha\cap\Phi=\{\pm\alpha\}
.

Figure 1
.
Two examples of crystallographic root systems. Type
\operatorname{A}_{2}
on the left, type
\operatorname{BC}_{2}
on the right. While
\operatorname{A}_{2}
is reduced,
\operatorname{BC}_{2}
is not.

Figure
1
gives examples of two crystallographic root systems, one of them reduced the other not. The
spherical Weyl group
W_{s}
of a root system is the subgroup of isometries of
V
generated by reflections along the hyperplanes
M_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\Phi
. A subset
\Delta\subseteq\Phi
is a
basis of
\Phi
, if it is a vector space basis of
V
such that all roots
\beta\in\Phi
can be written as
\beta=\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}\lambda_{\delta}\delta
with
\lambda_{\delta}\in\mathbb{Z}
for
\delta\in\Delta
and such that all
\lambda_{\delta}
have the same sign (all are non-negative or non-positive). Every root system has a basis and the spherical Weyl group acts transitively on the set of bases of
\Phi
. The cardinality of a basis
\Delta
is called the
rank
of the root system
\Phi
and coincides with
\dim(V)
.

The connected components of
V\setminus\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Phi}M_{\alpha}
are called
(open) chambers
. The spherical Weyl group acts simply transitively on the set of chambers. Given a basis
\Delta
, the
fundamental Weyl chamber of
\Delta
is

C_{0}(\Delta)=\left\{v\in V\colon\langle v,\alpha\rangle>0\ \text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta\right\}.

Conversely, every chamber
C
determines a basis
\Delta(C)
formed by those
\alpha\in\Phi
, that satisfy
\langle\alpha,v\rangle>0
for all
v\in C
and that cannot be written as a sum of other such elements of
\Phi
, see
[
Bou08
, VI§1.5]
. A basis
\Delta
determines the set of
positive roots

\Phi_{>0}\subseteq\Phi
given by positive integer combinations of elements in the basis. A basis thus determines a partial order on
\Phi
by
\alpha<\beta
if
\beta-\alpha\in\Phi_{>0}
. A total order on
\Phi
with the same positive elements can be obtained by choosing an order on the basis and extending it lexicographically to
\Phi\subseteq\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)
.

The scalar product defines an isomorphism of Euclidean vector spaces
V\cong V^{\star}
,
v\mapsto v^{\star}
by the defining property that
v^{\star}(w)=\langle v,w\rangle
for
v,w\in\Phi
, where
V^{\star}
is equipped with the scalar product
\langle v^{\star},w^{\star}\rangle\coloneqq\langle v,w\rangle
. The
coroot
1
1

1

Sometimes
2\alpha/\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle\in V
is called a coroot instead of its dual.
of

\alpha\in\Phi
is

\alpha^{\vee}\coloneqq 2\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\in V^{\star}.

The
coroot system

(\Phi^{\vee},V^{\star})
, where
\Phi^{\vee}\coloneqq\{\alpha^{\vee}\colon\alpha\in\Phi\}
is also a root system
[
Bou08
, VI§1.1]
, but
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\vee}
are not isomorphic in general. When
\Phi
is a crystallographic root system, there is a non-degenerate bilinear form

\displaystyle b\colon\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)\times\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi^{\vee})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})

\displaystyle\mapsto 2\frac{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}=\beta^{\vee}(\alpha)

taking values in
\mathbb{Z}
. Given a basis
\Delta=\{\delta_{1},,\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
, the matrix
(B_{ij})_{ij}\coloneqq b(\delta_{i},\delta_{j}^{\vee})
is called the
Cartan matrix
. Note that we have the following calculation rules, but in general
(\alpha+\beta)^{\vee}\neq\alpha^{\vee}+\beta^{\vee}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\Phi
be a crystallographic root system and
\alpha,\beta\in\Phi
. Then

(i)

r_{\beta}(\alpha)=\alpha-b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})\cdot\beta=\alpha-\beta^{\vee}(\alpha)\cdot\beta
,

(ii)

b(\alpha,\alpha^{\vee})=2
,

(iii)

(\alpha^{\vee})^{\star}=(\alpha^{\star})^{\vee}
,

(iv)

(\alpha^{\vee})^{\vee}=\alpha
.

Proof.

Item (i) is immediate from the definition of the reflection
r_{\beta}
, and (ii) is the statement that
\Phi
is crystallographic. For all
H\in\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi^{\vee})
we have

(\alpha^{\star})^{\vee}(H)=2\frac{(\alpha^{\star})^{\star}(H)}{\langle\alpha^{\star},\alpha^{\star}\rangle}=2\frac{\langle\alpha^{\star},H\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}=\langle\alpha^{\vee},H\rangle=(\alpha^{\vee})^{\star}(H),

so (iii) holds and then (iv) follows from

(\alpha^{\vee})^{\vee}=2\frac{(\alpha^{\vee})^{\star}}{\langle\alpha^{\vee},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}=2\frac{(\alpha^{\star})^{\vee}}{\langle 2\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle},2\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\rangle}=2\frac{2\frac{(\alpha^{\star})^{\star}}{\langle\alpha^{\star},\alpha^{\star}\rangle}}{4\frac{1}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle^{2}}\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}=(\alpha^{\star})^{\star}=\alpha.\qed

2.3.
Apartments

We introduce the model apartment
\mathbb{A}
. A more detailed introduction can be found in
[
Ben94
]
, and including the non-crystallographic case in
[
Sch09
]
. The root systems in our setting will arise from algebraic groups, and are therefore crystallographic, but possibly not reduced.

Let
\Lambda
be a non-trivial ordered abelian group. Let
(\Phi,V)
be a crystallographic root system and
\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)\subseteq V
its associated lattice. Since both
\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)
and
\Lambda
are
\mathbb{Z}
-modules, we can define the
model apartment

\mathbb{A}\coloneqq\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda.

For a basis
\Delta=\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
, the
\mathbb{Z}
-bilinear map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)\times\Lambda

\displaystyle\to\Lambda^{r}

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}z_{i}\delta_{i},\lambda\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto(z_{1}\lambda,\ldots,z_{r}\lambda)

extends to a
\mathbb{Z}
-module isomorphism

\mathbb{A}\cong\Lambda^{r}
, giving rise to the notation

\mathbb{A}\cong\left\{\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}\lambda_{\delta}\delta\colon\lambda_{\delta}\in\Lambda\right\}.

Note that if
\Lambda
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space, then we also have

\mathbb{A}=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Phi)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\Lambda.

The action of
W_{s}
on
\Phi
extends to an action on
\mathbb{A}
, fixing
0\in\mathbb{A}
. The apartment
\mathbb{A}
itself acts by translation on
\mathbb{A}
. Let
T\subseteq\mathbb{A}
be a subgroup of the translation group normalized by
W_{s}
, meaning that
wTw^{-1}=T
for all
w\in W_{s}
. Then
W_{a}=T\rtimes W
is called the
affine Weyl group
and acts on
\mathbb{A}
. If
T=\mathbb{A}
,
W_{a}
is called the
full affine Weyl group
. We denote the data of a model apartment together with an affine Weyl group as
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(\Phi,\Lambda,T)
since the root system
\Phi
, the ordered abelian group
\Lambda
and the subgroup of translations
T
fully determine
\mathbb{A}
and the affine Weyl group.

For any root
\alpha\in\Phi
, the reflection
r_{\alpha}\colon\Phi\to\Phi
extends to a
reflection

\displaystyle r_{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{A}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{A}

\displaystyle\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}\lambda_{\delta}\delta

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}\lambda_{\delta}r_{\alpha}(\delta)

and for any
w\in W_{a}
, the conjugates
w\circ r_{\alpha}\circ w^{-1}\in W_{a}
are called
affine reflections
. The bilinear form
b
extends naturally to the bilinear form

\displaystyle b\colon\mathbb{A}\times\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi^{\vee})

\displaystyle\to\Lambda

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}\lambda_{\delta}\delta,\sum_{\varepsilon\in\Delta}\mu_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{\vee}\right)

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\Delta}\mu_{\varepsilon}b(\delta,\varepsilon^{\vee})\lambda_{\delta}

but we notice that
b
can not be extended to all of
\mathbb{A}\times\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi^{\vee})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda
, since
\Lambda
may not have a multiplication. For
\alpha\in\Phi
and
\lambda\in\Lambda
we define the
affine wall

M_{\alpha,\lambda}\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon b(x,\alpha^{\vee})=\lambda\right\}

as well as the two
affine half-spaces

\displaystyle H_{\alpha,\lambda}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon b(x,\alpha^{\vee})\geq\lambda\right\}

\displaystyle H_{\alpha,\lambda}^{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon b(x,\alpha^{\vee})\leq\lambda\right\}

defined by
M_{\alpha,\lambda}
. The intersection of the positive half-spaces for all
\delta\in\Delta
is called the
fundamental Weyl chamber

C_{0}\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon b\left(x,\delta^{\vee}\right)\geq 0\text{ for all }\delta\in\Delta\right\}=\bigcap_{\delta\in\Delta}H_{\delta,0}^{+}.

The images of the fundamental Weyl chamber under the action of the affine Weyl group are called
sectors or chambers
.

The model apartment
\mathbb{A}
can be endowed with a
\Lambda
-metric. There are multiple ways to do this and we will describe one. Since
\mathbb{A}
may not admit a
\Lambda
-valued scalar product, we instead use the
W_{s}
-invariant
\Lambda
-valued norm
N\colon\mathbb{A}\to\Lambda
defined by

N(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi_{>0}}\left|b(x,\alpha^{\vee})\right|,

where
|\lambda|=\max\{\lambda,-\lambda\}
is the absolute value. Then

d(x,y)=N(x-y)

turns
\mathbb{A}
into a
\Lambda
-metric space. Note that when
x-y\in C_{0}
,

d(x,y)=b\left(x-y,\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi_{>0}}\alpha^{\vee}\right).

Note that the
\Lambda
-metric space
\mathbb{A}
may not be uniquely geodesic: there may be two or more distinct isometric embeddings of a
\Lambda
-interval with coinciding endpoints. Since
\Lambda
is just a group, the classical notion of convexity using linear combinations does not make sense. Instead we say that a subset
B\subseteq\mathbb{A}
is
W_{a}
-convex
, if it is a finite intersection of affine half-spaces. Figure
2
illustrates that
\mathbb{A}
may not be uniquely geodesic and that the union of all geodesics between two points is a
W_{a}
-convex set.

Figure 2
.
Given two points
p,q\in\mathbb{A}
, the interval
\{r\in~\mathbb{A}\colon d(p,r)+d(r,q)=d(p,q)\}
is a
W_{a}
-convex set defined by the intersection of four half-planes parallel to the walls in this example of type
\operatorname{A}_{2}
.

2.4.
Affine
\Lambda
-buildings

Let
\mathbb{A}
be an apartment of type
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(\Phi,\Lambda,T)
with affine Weyl group
W_{a}
as in the previous section. Let
X
be a set and
\mathscr{F}
a set of injective maps
\mathbb{A}\to X
. The set
\mathscr{F}
is called the
atlas
or
apartment system
and its elements are called the
charts
. The images
f(\mathbb{A})
for
f\in\mathscr{F}
are called
apartments
. The images of walls, half-spaces and chambers are called
walls, half-apartments
and
sectors
.

The pair
(X,\mathscr{F})
is called a
generalized affine building
or a
affine
\Lambda
-building
of type
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(\Phi,\Lambda,T)
if the following six axioms are satisfied. Axioms (A4) and (A6) are illustrated in Figures
3
and
4
.

(A1)

For all
f\in\mathscr{F}
,
w\in W_{a}
,
f\circ w\in\mathscr{F}
.

(A2)

For all
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathscr{F}
, the set
B=f^{-1}\left(f(\mathbb{A})\cap f^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})\right)
is
W_{a}
-convex and there is a
w\in W_{a}
such that

f|_{B}=f^{\prime}\circ w|_{B}.

(A3)

For all
x,y\in X
, there is a
f\in\mathscr{F}
such that
x,y\in f(\mathbb{A})
.

(A4)

For any sectors
s_{1},s_{2}\subseteq X
there are subsectors
s_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq s_{1},s_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq s_{2}
such that there is an
f\in\mathscr{F}
with
s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq f(\mathbb{A})
.

(A5)

For every
x\in X
and
f\in\mathscr{F}
with
x\in f(\mathbb{A})
there is a distance diminishing retraction
r_{x,f}\colon X\to f(\mathbb{A})
such that
f^{-1}(\{x\})=\{x\}
.

(A6)

For
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}\in\mathscr{F}
, if
f_{i}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{j}(\mathbb{A})
are half-apartments for
i\neq j
, then
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})\neq\emptyset
.

Figure 3
.
Axiom (A4) states that while arbitrary sectors
s_{1},s_{2}
may not lie in a common flat, they contain subsectors
s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}
contained in a common flat
f(\mathbb{A})
.

Figure 4
.
Axiom (A6) states that whenever three apartments intersect pairwise in a half-apartment, then there is at least one point in the common intersection of all three.

The distance in axiom (A5) is the function
d\colon X\times X\to\Lambda
induced from the distance on the model apartment
\mathbb{A}
, whose existence follows from axioms (A1), (A2) and (A3): for any two points
x,y\in X
we use axiom (A3) to find
f\in\mathscr{F}
such that
x,y\in f(\mathbb{A})
and define
d(x,y)=d(f^{-1}(x),f^{-1}(y))\in\Lambda
. This is well defined, by axioms (A1) and (A2). A
d
-diminishing retraction is then a function
r\colon X\to f(\mathbb{A})
satisfying
r(y)=y
for all
y\in f(\mathbb{A})
and

d(r(x),r(y))\leq d(x,y)

for all
x,y\in X
. Note that while axioms (A1) - (A3) can be used to define a symmetric positive definite function
d
, axiom (A5) can be used to show that
d
satisfies the triangle inequality. As shown in
[
BSS14
]
, in the presence of the other five axioms, axiom (A5) is equivalent to the triangle inequality. In fact,
[
BSS14
]
contains a number of collections of axioms that characterize affine
\Lambda
-buildings. We will use the following characterization.

Theorem 2.3

(Theorem 3.1,
[
BSS14
]
)
.

Let
(X,\mathscr{F})
be a set with an atlas such that the axioms (A1), (A2), (A3) and (A4), as well as

(TI)

The function
d
induced from the distance in apartments satisfies the triangle inequality.

and the exchange condition

(EC)

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{F}
, if
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
is a half-apartment, then there exists
f_{3}\in\mathscr{F}
such that
f_{i}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})
are half-apartments for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Moreover
f_{3}(\mathbb{A})
is the symmetric difference of
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})
and
f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
together with the boundary wall of
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
.

are satisfied. Then
(X,\mathscr{F})
is an affine
\Lambda
-building.

3.
Valued real closed fields

For an introduction to real closed fields, valued fields and real algebraic geometry we recommend
[
BCR98
]
, see also Section
of
[
App25
]
. A
real closed field
is an ordered field with the properties that

(a)

every positive element has a square root, and

(b)

every odd-degree polynomial has a zero.

An ordered field is called
Archimedean
if every element is bounded by a natural number. A major tool when working with real closed fields is the following transfer principle from model theory.

Theorem 3.1

(Transfer principle,
[
BCR98
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
be real closed fields. Let
\varphi
be a sentence in first-order logic for ordered fields with parameters in
\mathbb{F}\cap\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
. Then
\varphi
is true for
\mathbb{F}
if and only if
\varphi
is true for
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
, formally
\mathbb{F}\models\varphi\iff\mathbb{F}^{\prime}\models\varphi
.

3.1.
Valuations

A subring
O\subseteq\mathbb{F}
of an ordered field
\mathbb{F}
is an
order convex subring
, if for all
a,b\in O,\ c\in\mathbb{F},\ a\leq c\leq b
implies
c\in O
. Note that every order convex subring is in particular a valuation ring: for all
a\in\mathbb{F}
, we have
a\in O
or
a^{-1}\in O
. Let
(\Lambda,+)
be an ordered abelian group. A
valuation
on an ordered field
\mathbb{F}
is a map
v\colon\mathbb{F}\to\Lambda\cup\{\infty\}
which satisfies for all
a,b\in\mathbb{F}

(1)

v(a)=\infty
if and only if
a=0
.

(2)

v(ab)=v(a)+v(b)
.

(3)

v(a+b)\geq\min\{v(a),v(b)\}
.

We say that the valuation is
order compatible
, if
(-v)(a)\geq(-v)(b)
whenever
a\geq b\geq 0
. We will often use the same letter for
v
and
v|_{\mathbb{F}_{>0}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{>0}\to\Lambda
. We will often be more interested in
(-v)
than in
v
, as
(-v)
is order preserving (when restricted to
\mathbb{F}_{>0}
). There is a correspondence between order convex valuation rings and order compatible valuations.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
Kru39
]
)
.

Every order convex valuation ring
O\subseteq\mathbb{F}
gives rise to an order compatible valuation

\displaystyle v\colon\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle\to\Lambda\cup\{\infty\},

where
\Lambda\cong\mathbb{F}^{\times}/O^{\times}
with the order given by
-v(a)\leq-v(b)
for all
0\leq a\leq b\in\mathbb{F}
. On the other hand, every order compatible valuation gives rise to an order convex valuation ring

O\coloneqq\{a\in\mathbb{F}\colon v(a)\geq 0\}.

Real closed fields always admit order compatible valuation rings (sometimes many), but the only order compatible valuation on an Archimedean real closed field is the trivial valuation. The ordered abelian group
\Lambda
of a valuation of a real closed field is always divisible, and hence a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space.

3.2.
Residue field

If
O
is a valuation ring with maximal ideal

J\coloneqq\{a\in O\colon v(a)>0\}=O\setminus O^{\times}

then
k\coloneqq O/J
is called the
residue field
. When
\mathbb{F}
is real closed, so is
k

[
EP05
, Theorem 4.3.7]
. To an algebraic group
G
and a real closed field
\mathbb{F}
we will associate an affine
\Lambda
-building
\mathcal{B}
. In Section
6
we will show that the building at infinity is the spherical building associated to
G(\mathbb{F})
and the residue building is the spherical building associated to
G(k)
.

3.3.
Examples

The real numbers
\mathbb{R}
and the real algebraic numbers
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\operatorname{rc}}
are Archimedean real closed fields.
The field of
Puiseux series
over some real closed field
\mathbb{F}

\overline{\mathbb{F}(X)}^{\operatorname{rc}}\coloneqq\left\{\sum_{k=-\infty}^{k_{0}}c_{k}X^{\frac{k}{m}}\,\colon\,k_{0},m\in\mathbb{Z},\,m>0,\,c_{k}\in\mathbb{F},\,c_{k_{0}}\neq 0\right\},

is a non-Archimedean real closed field, where the usual order on
\mathbb{F}
is extended by
X>r
for all
r\in\mathbb{F}

[
BCR98
]
. An order compatible valuation
v\colon\overline{\mathbb{F}(X)}^{\operatorname{rc}}\to\Lambda\cup\{\infty\}=\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}
is given by the degree

\displaystyle v\left(\sum_{k=-\infty}^{k_{0}}c_{k}X^{k/m}\right)=-\frac{k_{0}}{m}.

Analogous to the algebraic closure of fields, every ordered field
\mathbb{K}
admits a
real closure

\overline{\mathbb{K}}^{\operatorname{rc}}
, the intersection of all real closed fields containing
\mathbb{K}
. The real algebraic numbers
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}^{\operatorname{rc}}
are the real closure of the rationals and the Puiseux-series are the real closure of the ordered field of rational functions
\mathbb{F}(X)
.

Generalizing slightly, we may consider the field of rational functions
\mathbb{F}(X,Y)
with two variables, with the order where
X>Y^{n}>r
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in\mathbb{F}
. The multi-degree gives a valuation
v\colon\mathbb{F}(X,Y)\to\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\infty\}
with lexicographical ordering, and the real closure
\overline{\mathbb{F}(X,Y)}^{\operatorname{rc}}
is a real closed field with valuation group
\Lambda\cong\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}
with lexicographical ordering.

A
non-principal ultrafilter on
\mathbb{Z}
is a function
\omega\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{Z})\to\{0,1\}
that satisfies

(1)

\omega(\emptyset)=0
,
\omega(\mathbb{Z})=1
,

(2)

if
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
A\cap B=\emptyset
, then
\omega(A\cup B)=\omega(A)+\omega(B)
,

(3)

all finite subsets
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
satisfy
\omega(A)=0
.

Ultrafilters can be thought of as finitely-additive probability measures that only take values in
0
and
1
. The existence of non-principal ultrafilters is equivalent to the axiom of choice
[
Hal66
]
. For a given ultrafilter
\omega
, we define the
hyperreal numbers

\mathbb{R}_{\omega}
to be the equivalence classes of infinite sequences
\mathbb{R}_{\omega}=\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}/\!\!\sim
, where
x=(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\sim y=(y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
if
\omega(\{i\in\mathbb{N}\colon x_{i}\neq y_{i}\})=0
or
\omega(\{i\in\mathbb{N}\colon x_{i}=y_{i}\})=1
. We define addition and multiplication componentwise, the multiplicative inverse is obtained by taking the inverses of all non-zero entries, turning
\mathbb{F}_{\omega}
into a field. Considering constant sequences, the real numbers are a subfield of
\mathbb{R}_{\omega}
. The hyperreals are an ordered field with respect to the order defined by
[(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}]\leq[(y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}]
if and only if
\omega(\{i\in\mathbb{N}\colon x_{i}\leq y_{i}\})=1
. The hyperreals are real closed, since
\mathbb{R}
is. One can check that the hyperreals do not admit a non-trivial valuation to a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
: any valuation group for the hyperreals has infinite rank. The hyperreals are non-Archimedean, since the equivalence class containing
(1,2,3,\ldots)
is an
infinite
element, meaning it is larger than any natural number.

Let
b\in\mathbb{R}_{\omega}
be an infinite element. Then

O_{b}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\omega}\colon|x|<b^{m}\text{ for some }m\in\mathbb{Z}\}

is an order convex subring of
\mathbb{R}_{\omega}
with maximal ideal

J_{b}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathbb{R}_{\omega}\colon|x|<b^{m}\text{ for all }m\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

The
Robinson field
associated to the non-principal ultrafilter
\omega
and the infinite element
b
is the quotient
\mathbb{R}_{\omega,b}\coloneqq O_{b}/J_{b}

[
Rob96
]
. The Robinson field is a non-Archimedean real closed field. Note that
[b]\in\mathbb{R}_{\omega,b}
is a
big
element, meaning that for all
a\in\mathbb{R}_{\omega,b}
there is an
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
a<b^{n}
.

Non-Archimedean ordered fields
\mathbb{F}
with big elements admit an order compatible valuation
v\colon\mathbb{F}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
by letting
v(a)
be the real number defined by the Dedekind cut

\displaystyle A_{a}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\frac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q}\colon b^{p}\leq a^{q},q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},p\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

\displaystyle B_{a}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\frac{p}{q}\in\mathbb{Q}\colon b^{p}\geq a^{q},q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},p\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

for
a\in\mathbb{F}
and
b\in\mathbb{F}
a big element
[
Bru88b
]
. Note that when
\mathbb{F}
is Archimedean, every element
b>1
is big and we can still define as above
v(a)=-\log_{b}|a|
, which then is the usual logarithm with base
b
. However
v
is not a valuation in our sense, since it does not satisfy the strong triangle inequality, condition (3) in the definition.

4.
Definition of the building
\mathcal{B}

For background on algebraic groups over real closed fields, see
[
App25
]
. Let
\mathbb{K}
and
\mathbb{F}
be real closed fields such that
\mathbb{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\cap\mathbb{F}
. Often we assume
\mathbb{F}
to be non-Archimedean with order compatible valuation
v\colon\mathbb{F}\to\Lambda\cup\{\infty\}
. Let
G<\operatorname{SL}_{n}
be a semisimple connected self-adjoint algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-group and
S
a maximal
\mathbb{K}
-split torus that satisfies
s=s{{}^{\mathsf{T}}\!}
for all
s\in S
. Let
A_{\mathbb{F}}
be the semialgebraic extension of the semialgebraic connected component of
S_{\mathbb{K}}
containing the identity and let
K\coloneqq G\cap\operatorname{SO}_{n}
.

For
\mathfrak{a}=\operatorname{Lie}(A_{\mathbb{R}})
, let
\Sigma\subseteq\mathfrak{a}^{\star}
be the root system whose elements
\alpha\in\Sigma
correspond to
\mathbb{K}
-roots
\chi_{\alpha}\in\vphantom{\Phi}_{\mathbb{K}}\Phi\subseteq\hat{S}
, see Section
in
[
App25
]
. Then
W_{s}=\vphantom{W}_{\mathbb{K}}W
is its spherical Weyl group. After choosing a basis
\Delta\subseteq\Sigma
we let
U
be the unipotent group associated to the positive root spaces and

A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}\coloneqq\left\{a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\chi_{\alpha}(a)\geq 1\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta\right\}.

4.1.
Non-standard symmetric spaces

In the theory of symmetric spaces,

P_{\mathbb{R}}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\colon x=x{{}^{\mathsf{T}}\!},\,\det(x)=1,\,x\text{ is positive definite}\right\}

is a model for the symmetric space of non-compact type associated to
\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
. The group
\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
acts transitively on
P_{\mathbb{R}}
by

g.x=gxg^{T}.

for
g\in\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
,
x\in P_{\mathbb{R}}
. The orbit
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}=G_{\mathbb{R}}.\operatorname{Id}\subseteq P_{\mathbb{R}}
is a closed subset and a model for the symmetric space associated to
G_{\mathbb{R}}
. We note that
P
is a semialgebraic set defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and consider its
\mathbb{F}
-extension
P_{\mathbb{F}}
. The action is algebraic, so the orbit can be semialgebraically extended to

\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}=G_{\mathbb{F}}.P_{\mathbb{F}}.

When
\mathbb{F}
is non-Archimedean, we call
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
the
non-standard symmetric space associated to
G_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

(a)

The group
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
.

(b)

The stabilizer of
\operatorname{Id}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
is
K_{\mathbb{F}}
.

(c)

For any pair
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
there is a
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g.x=\operatorname{Id}
and
g.y
lies in the non-standard closed Weyl chamber

A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}.\operatorname{Id}=\left\{a.\operatorname{Id}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\chi_{\alpha}(a)\geq 1\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Sigma\right\}.

Proof.

Transitivity and stabilizer of
\operatorname{Id}
follow directly from the definitions. Use transitivity obtain
h\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
h.x=\operatorname{Id}
. Use transitivity again to obtain
h^{\prime}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
h^{\prime}.h.y=\operatorname{Id}
. Now decompose
h^{\prime}=kak^{\prime}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}=K_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}K_{\mathbb{F}}
using the Cartan decomposition
[
App25
, Theorem
]
, where we may assume
a^{-1}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}
after applying an element of the spherical Weyl group
W_{s}
. Setting
g=k^{\prime}h
now results in the claimed

\displaystyle g.x

\displaystyle=k^{\prime}h.x=k^{\prime}.\operatorname{Id}=\operatorname{Id},

\displaystyle g.y

\displaystyle=k^{\prime}h.y=k^{\prime}(h^{\prime})^{-1}.\operatorname{Id}=a^{-1}k^{-1}.\operatorname{Id}=a^{-1}.\operatorname{Id}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}.\operatorname{Id}.\qed

4.2.
The pseudo-distance

The symmetric space
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
admits an explicit distance formula. Here we mimic this process to define a pseudo-distance on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
. Let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
be two points. We will first send
x
and
y
to a common flat, on which we define a multiplicative norm
N_{\mathbb{F}}
. In the real case, the logarithm would then be applied to obtain an additive distance. For the non-standard symmetric space we instead use the valuation
v\colon\mathbb{F}_{>0}\to\Lambda
.

By Proposition
4.1
(a), for
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
there are
g,h\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
x=g.\operatorname{Id}
,
y=h.\operatorname{Id}
. We use the Cartan decomposition
[
App25
, Theorem
]
to write
g^{-1}h=kak^{\prime}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}=K_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}K_{\mathbb{F}}
, where
a
can be chosen to lie in
A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}
and is then unique. This gives a Weyl-chamber valued distance on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
, which we call the
Cartan projection
.

Lemma 4.2
.

The Cartan-projection

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\to A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto a

is well-defined and invariant under the action of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
. For all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
,
\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(y,x)
is in the Weyl-group orbit of
\delta(x,y)^{-1}
.

Proof.

Assume
x=g.\operatorname{Id}=\overline{g}.\operatorname{Id}
and
y=h.\operatorname{Id}=\overline{h}.\operatorname{Id}
for some
g,\overline{g},h,\overline{h}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
. By Proposition
4.1
(b),
g^{-1}\overline{g}\in K_{\mathbb{F}}
and
h^{-1}\overline{h}\in K_{\mathbb{F}}
. Let
a,\overline{a}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}
be the unique elements satisfying
g^{-1}h\in K_{\mathbb{F}}aK_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\overline{g}^{-1}\overline{h}=\overline{k}\overline{a}\overline{k}^{\prime}\in K_{\mathbb{F}}\overline{a}K_{\mathbb{F}}
from the Cartan decomposition
[
App25
, Theorem
]
. Then

\overline{k}\overline{a}\overline{k}=\overline{g}^{-1}\overline{h}\in K_{\mathbb{F}}g^{-1}hK_{\mathbb{F}}=K_{\mathbb{F}}aK_{\mathbb{F}},

so
\overline{a}=a
and
\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(x,y)=a
is well-defined. The
G_{\mathbb{F}}
-invariance holds because for
t\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
, the distance between
t.g.\operatorname{Id}
and
t.h.\operatorname{Id}
is calculated from
(tg)^{-1}(th)=g^{-1}h
and thus agrees with the distance between
x
and
y
. If
\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(x,y)=a
, then
\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(y,x)
is defined by
h^{-1}g\in K_{\mathbb{F}}\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(y,x)K_{\mathbb{F}}
. Thus

K_{\mathbb{F}}\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(y,x)K_{\mathbb{F}}\ni h^{-1}g=(g^{-1}h)^{-1}\in K_{\mathbb{F}}\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(x,y)^{-1}K_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq K_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}K_{\mathbb{F}},

and since the Cartan decomposition is unique up to the action of the spherical Weyl group,
\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(y,x)
is in the orbit of
\delta(x,y)^{-1}
.
∎

We use a basis
\Delta
of the root system
\Sigma
to define a notion of positive roots
\Sigma_{>0}
. For
\alpha\in\Sigma
, let
\chi_{\alpha}\colon A_{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\times}
be the corresponding (
\mathbb{R}
-points of the) algebraic characters. We define a continuous, semialgebraic
W_{s}
-invariant map

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{R}}\colon A_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{\times}

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\max\left\{\chi_{\alpha}(a),\chi_{\alpha}(a)^{-1}\right\}

which is a multiplicative norm, meaning that for all
a,b\in A_{\mathbb{R}}

(1)

N_{\mathbb{R}}(a)\geq 1
and
N_{\mathbb{R}}(a)=1
if and only if
a=\operatorname{Id}
,

(2)

N_{\mathbb{R}}(ab)\leq N_{\mathbb{R}}(a)N_{\mathbb{R}}(b)
.

We call
N_{\mathbb{R}}
the
semialgebraic norm
2
2

2

There are many continuous semialgebraic multiplicative norms satisfying (1) and (2), but as norms on finite dimensional vector spaces, they are equivalent and it suffices for our purposes to fix
N_{\mathbb{R}}
.
.
Since
N_{\mathbb{R}}
is semialgebraic, we can extend it to a map
N_{\mathbb{F}}\colon A_{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathbb{F}^{\times}
which is still a
W_{s}
-invariant multiplicative norm satisfying (1) and (2) by the transfer principle and
N_{\mathbb{F}}
is given by the same formula involving the characters.
For
\mathbb{F}
non-Archimedean, we now use the Cartan projection
\delta_{\mathbb{F}}
together with the semialgebraic norm
N_{\mathbb{F}}
and the valuation
v\colon\mathbb{F}_{>0}\to\Lambda
to define

\displaystyle d\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\to\Lambda

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto(-v)(N_{\mathbb{F}}(\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(x,y))).

We will show in Theorem
4.5
that
d
is a pseudo-distance on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
. The pseudo-distance
d
fails to be positive definite due to the fact that
v
is not injective. The proof of the triangle inequality uses Kostant’s convexity theorem and the Iwasawa retraction

\displaystyle\rho\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\to A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}

\displaystyle g.\operatorname{Id}=uak.\operatorname{Id}

\displaystyle\mapsto a.\operatorname{Id}.

using the Iwasawa-decomposition
G_{\mathbb{F}}=U_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}K_{\mathbb{F}}

[
App25
, Theorem
]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For all
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
,
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
,
\rho(a.x)=a.\rho(x)
.

Proof.

Let
g=ua^{\prime}k\in G_{\mathbb{F}}=U_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}K_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g.\operatorname{Id}=x
. By
[
App25
, Proposition
]
,
aua^{-1}\in U_{\mathbb{F}}
, so

\displaystyle\rho(a.x)

\displaystyle=\rho(aua^{\prime}k.\operatorname{Id})=\rho((aua^{-1}aa^{\prime}.\operatorname{Id})=aa^{\prime}.\operatorname{Id}=a.\rho(ua^{\prime}k.\operatorname{Id})=a.\rho(x).

∎

We use Kostant’s convexity theorem to prove that
\rho
is a
d
-diminishing retraction.

Theorem 4.4
.

The map
\rho\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\to A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}
is a
d
-diminishing

\forall x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\colon d(\rho(x),\rho(y))\leq d(x,y),

retraction to
A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\rho
is a retraction, meaning that
\forall a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
,
\rho(a.\operatorname{Id})=a.\operatorname{Id}
. Let
a_{\mathbb{F}}(g)=a_{\mathbb{F}}(uak)=a
denote the
A_{\mathbb{F}}
-component of
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
as in Section
of
[
App25
]
on Kostant convexity. To show that
\rho
is
d
-diminishing, we first claim that for all

b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}

and for all
k\in K_{\mathbb{F}}

d(\operatorname{Id},\rho(kb.\operatorname{Id}))\leq d(\operatorname{Id},b.\operatorname{Id}).

For
b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}
the set

S_{\mathbb{F}}^{b}=\left\{a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\colon a=a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb)\text{ for some }k\in K_{\mathbb{F}}\right\}

is semialgebraic and since over
\mathbb{R}
,
S_{\mathbb{R}}^{b}
is closed under the action of the spherical Weyl group (this is a consequence of the real Kostant convexity theorem
[
Kos73
, Theorem 4.1]
). The statement
W_{s}(S_{\mathbb{R}}^{b})\subseteq S_{\mathbb{R}}^{b}
can be formulated as a first-order formula, so
S_{\mathbb{F}}^{b}
is also closed under the action of
W_{s}
. Note that
\rho(kb.\operatorname{Id})=a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb).\operatorname{Id}
. While
a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb)
may not lie in
A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}
, there is a
w\in W
such that
w(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb))\in A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}
. We apply
[
App25
, Theorem
]
to
w(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb))\in A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}\cap S_{\mathbb{F}}^{b}
to get

\chi_{\gamma_{i}}(w(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb)))\leq\chi_{\gamma_{i}}(b)

for all
\gamma_{i}
described in
[
App25
, Section
]
. By
[
App25
, Lemma
]
for every
\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}
there are non-negative rational numbers
n_{\alpha i}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
such that

\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{r}n_{\alpha i}\gamma_{i}.

We can now prove

\displaystyle(N_{\mathbb{F}}\circ\delta_{\mathbb{F}})(\operatorname{Id},\rho(kb.\operatorname{Id}))

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\max\left\{\chi_{\alpha}(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb)),\chi_{\alpha}(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb))^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}}\max\left\{\chi_{\alpha}(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb))^{\pm 2}\right\}=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}}\chi_{\alpha}(w(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb)))^{2}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\chi_{\gamma_{i}}(w(a_{\mathbb{F}}(kb)))^{2n_{\alpha i}}\leq\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\chi_{\gamma_{i}}(b)^{2n_{\alpha i}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\chi_{\alpha}(b)=(N_{\mathbb{F}}\circ\delta_{\mathbb{F}})(\operatorname{Id},b.\operatorname{Id}),

where we used that
n_{\alpha i}\geq 0
, so after applying
(-v)
we proved the claim.

Now let
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
arbitrary. By Proposition
4.1
(c) and
[
App25
, Theorem
]
, we can find
g=uak\in G_{\mathbb{F}}=U_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}K_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
x=g.\operatorname{Id}
and
y=g.b.\operatorname{Id}
for some
b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
. Now we use Lemma
4.3
and the above to conclude

\displaystyle d(\rho(x),\rho(y))

\displaystyle=d(\rho(uak.\operatorname{Id}),\rho(uakb.\operatorname{Id}))=d(a.\operatorname{Id},\rho(akb.\operatorname{Id}))

\displaystyle=d(\operatorname{Id},a^{-1}.\rho(akb.\operatorname{Id}))=d(\operatorname{Id},\rho(kb.\operatorname{Id}))

\displaystyle\leq d(\operatorname{Id},b.\operatorname{Id})=d(g.\operatorname{Id},g.b.\operatorname{Id})=d(x,y).\qed

Theorem 4.5
.

The function
d\colon\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}\to\Lambda
is a pseudo-distance.

Proof.

We note that by definition,
N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)=N_{\mathbb{F}}(a^{-1})
for all
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
. By Weyl group invariance and the last part of Lemma
4.2
we then obtain
N_{\mathbb{F}}(\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(x,y))=N_{\mathbb{F}}(\delta_{\mathbb{F}}(y,x))
for all
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
, whence
d
is symmetric.
Since
N_{\mathbb{F}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{\geq 1}
,
(-v)(1)=0
and
-v
is order-preserving,
d
is positive. It is also clear that
d(x,x)=0
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
. It remains to show that the triangle inequality holds. We start by analyzing the distance on the non-standard maximal flats
A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}
. Let
a,b,c\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
, then we can use property (2) of the semialgebraic norm
N_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathbb{F}_{>0}
to deduce

\displaystyle d(a.\operatorname{Id},b.\operatorname{Id})

\displaystyle=d(\operatorname{Id},a^{-1}b.\operatorname{Id})=(-v)(N_{\mathbb{F}}(a^{-1}b))

\displaystyle=(-v)(N_{\mathbb{F}}(a^{-1}cc^{-1}b))\leq-(v)(N_{\mathbb{F}}(a^{-1}c)N_{\mathbb{F}}(c^{-1}b))

\displaystyle=-v(N_{\mathbb{F}}(a^{-1}c))+(-v)(N_{\mathbb{F}}(c^{-1}b))

\displaystyle=d(a.\operatorname{Id},c.\operatorname{Id})+d(c.\operatorname{Id},b.\operatorname{Id}),

which settles the triangle inequality for points in
A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}
. For the general case we use the Iwasawa retraction from Theorem
4.4
, as suggested in
[
KT02
, Lemma 1.2]
. Let
x,y,z\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
. By Proposition
4.1
(c) there is a
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
g.x,g.y\in A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}
. Then

\displaystyle d(x,y)

\displaystyle=d(g.x,g.y)=d(\rho(g.x),\rho(g.y))

\displaystyle\leq d(\rho(g.x),\rho(g.z))+d(\rho(g.z),\rho(g.y))

\displaystyle\leq d(g.x,g.z)+d(g.z,g.y)=d(x,z)+d(z,y)

concludes the proof.
∎

4.3.
The building

By Theorem
4.5
, the non-standard symmetric space
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
admits a
\Lambda
-pseudometric. We consider the quotient

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}/\!\sim

where
x\sim y\in\mathcal{B}
when
d(x,y)=0\in\Lambda
. We denote the induced
\Lambda
-metric on
\mathcal{B}
by the same letter
d
. We note that
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts by isometries on
\mathcal{B}
. In Section
5
we will show that the
\Lambda
-metric space
\mathcal{B}
admits the structure of an affine
\Lambda
-building in certain cases, see Theorem
5.1
.
Before we start checking the axioms of affine
\Lambda
-buildings, we investigate the apartment structure in Section
4.4
and stabilizers in Section
4.6
.

4.4.
The model apartment

Over the reals, the orbit
A_{\mathbb{R}}.\operatorname{Id}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{R}}
is a maximal flat in the symmetric space
\mathcal{X}
. We take a closer look at the group
A_{\mathbb{F}}
and its orbit
A_{\mathbb{F}}.\operatorname{Id}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\mathbb{F}}
to define a space
A_{\Lambda}
which will play the role of the model apartment
\mathbb{A}
.
Let
O
be an order convex valuation ring of the non-Archimedean real closed field
\mathbb{F}
and
(-v)\colon\mathbb{F}_{>0}\to\Lambda
the associated order preserving valuation. We define the group

A_{\Lambda}\coloneqq A_{\mathbb{F}}/\{a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\colon N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)\in O\}.

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
4.12
which states that
A_{\Lambda}
can be given the structure of a model apartment
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(\Sigma^{\vee},\Lambda,A_{\Lambda})
, as defined in Section
2.3
. We also describe walls, half-apartments, sectors and the distance function in terms of
A_{\mathbb{F}}
.

From
[
App25
, Section
]
we recall that the root system
\Sigma
of the real Lie group
G_{\mathbb{R}}
can be identified with the root system
\vphantom{\Phi}{}_{\mathbb{K}}\Phi
of characters, where associated to every root
\alpha\in\Sigma
there is a character
\chi_{\alpha}\colon A_{\mathbb{F}}\to\mathbb{F}_{>0}
. Similarly, the coroot system
\Sigma^{\vee}
can be identified with the coroots
X_{\star}(S)
, where associated to every root
\alpha
we obtain a one-parameter subgroup
t_{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{F}_{>0}\to A_{\mathbb{F}}
. In fact there are algebraic characters
\chi_{\eta}
and one-parameter subgroups
t_{\eta}
for every
\eta\in\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma)
and semialgebraic characters and one-parameter subgroups for
\eta\in\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)
.

Formally
\Sigma\subseteq\mathfrak{a}^{\star}
where
\mathfrak{a}
is a maximal abelian subalgebra of
\mathfrak{p}
in a Cartan decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{p}
. Thus
\Sigma^{\vee}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}
, using the canonical identification
(\mathfrak{a}^{\star})^{\star}\cong\mathfrak{a}
. Recall that every crystallographic root system
\Phi
comes with a non-degenerate bilinear form

\displaystyle b\colon\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi)\times\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Phi^{\vee})

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})

\displaystyle\mapsto 2\frac{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}=\beta^{\vee}(\alpha).

The bilinear forms
b
of
\Sigma
and
b^{\vee}
of
\Sigma^{\vee}
are not symmetric, but they are the transposes of each other in the following sense.

Lemma 4.6
.

For all
\alpha,\beta\in\Sigma
,

\alpha(\beta^{\vee})=\beta^{\vee}(\alpha)=b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})=b^{\vee}(\beta^{\vee},\alpha),

where we identified
(\alpha^{\vee})^{\vee}=\alpha\in\Sigma
.

Proof.

This is a direct computation using
\langle\beta^{\star},\alpha^{\star}\rangle=\langle\beta,\alpha\rangle
,

\displaystyle b^{\vee}\left(\beta^{\vee},(\alpha^{\vee})^{\vee}\right)

\displaystyle=2\frac{\langle\beta^{\vee},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}{\langle\alpha^{\vee},\alpha^{\vee}\rangle}=2\frac{\left\langle 2\frac{\beta^{\star}}{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle},2\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\right\rangle}{\left\langle 2\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle},2\frac{\alpha^{\star}}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\right\rangle}=2\frac{\langle\beta,\alpha\rangle}{\langle\beta,\beta\rangle}=b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee}).\qed

Proposition 4.7
.

For all
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma)
and
x\in\mathbb{F}_{>0}
, we have

\chi_{\alpha}(t_{\beta}(x))=x^{b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})}.

Proof.

Over the reals, we have by
[
App25
, Lemma
]
that for all
x=e^{s}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{\alpha}\left(t_{\beta}\left(e^{s}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\alpha}\left(\exp\left(sx_{\beta}\right)\right)=e^{\alpha(sx_{\beta})}=\left(e^{s}\right)^{\alpha(x_{\beta})}=x^{b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})}

where
x_{\beta}:=\beta^{\vee}:=2\beta^{\star}/\langle\beta,\beta\rangle\in\Sigma^{\vee}
. The statement is a first-order formula, hence by the transfer principle the statement also holds for
\mathbb{F}
.
∎

The characters and one-parameter subgroups descend to
A_{\Lambda}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

For all
\alpha,\beta\in\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma)
, the characters
\chi_{\alpha}
and the one-parameter subgroups
t_{\beta}
descend to group homomorphisms
\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}\colon A_{\Lambda}\to\Lambda
and
\overline{t}_{\beta}\colon\Lambda\to A_{\Lambda}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{>0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\mathbb{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{>0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{t}_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}}

commutes and such that

\chi_{\alpha}\circ t_{\beta}(\lambda)=b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})\cdot\lambda
for every
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

We denote by
\pi\colon A_{\mathbb{F}}\to A_{\Lambda}=A_{\mathbb{F}}/\{a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\colon N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)\in O\}
the projection. We first show that
\overline{t}_{\beta}\colon\Lambda\to A_{\Lambda},\,(-v)(x)\mapsto\pi(t_{\beta}(x))
is well defined: we have to show that if
(-v)(x)=0
, then
N(t_{\beta}(x))\in O
. Indeed, let
x\in\mathbb{F}_{>0}
such that
(-v)(x)=0
, so
x\in O^{\times}
. Then in particular
\chi_{\gamma}(t_{\beta}(x))=x^{b(\gamma,\beta^{\vee})}\in O^{\times}
for all
\gamma\in\Sigma
and thus

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{F}}(t_{\beta}(x))

\displaystyle=\prod_{\gamma\in\Sigma}\max\left\{\chi_{\gamma}(t_{\beta}(x)),\chi_{\gamma}(t_{\beta}(x))^{-1}\right\}\in O.

To show that
\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}\colon A_{\Lambda}\to\Lambda,\,\pi(a)\mapsto-v(\chi_{\alpha}(a))
is well-defined, we have to show that if
N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)\in O
, then
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))=0
. Indeed, if
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)\in O
, then
\chi_{\gamma}(a)\in O
for all
\gamma\in\Sigma
since

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\gamma\in\Sigma}\max\left\{\chi_{\gamma}(a),\chi_{\gamma}(a)^{-1}\right\}\in O

is a product of elements that are larger than
1
. Since
\alpha\in\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Sigma)
,
\chi_{\alpha}(a)
is a product of
\chi_{\gamma}(a)\in O
and hence in
O
.

The maps
\overline{t}_{\beta}
and
\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}
were defined so that the diagrams commute. For
\lambda\in\Lambda
, we can find
x\in\mathbb{F}_{>0}
such that
(-v)(x)=\lambda
. Then

\displaystyle\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}\circ\overline{t}_{\beta}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}(\pi(t_{\beta}(x)))=(-v)\left(\chi_{\alpha}\circ t_{\beta}(x)\right)

\displaystyle=-v\left(x^{b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})}\right)=b(\alpha,\beta^{\vee})\cdot(-v)(x).\qed

Our goal is to use the characters and one-parameter subgroups to identify
A_{\mathbb{F}}
with the model apartment
\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma^{\vee})
. As
\mathbb{F}
is real closed, we can view
\mathbb{F}_{>0}
as a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space with neutral element
1
and scalar multiplication given by potentiation.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
L\coloneqq\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)
. The map
\psi\colon A_{\mathbb{F}}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\mathbb{F}_{>0})
that sends
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
to
\gamma\mapsto\chi_{\gamma}(a)
is a group isomorphism. For all
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\Delta
, there are rational numbers
q_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that

a=\prod_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\Delta}t_{\delta}(\chi_{\delta^{\prime}}(a))^{q_{\delta,\delta^{\prime}}}

for all
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Proof.

It is clear that
\psi
is a well defined group homomorphism. From dimension reasons over the reals
\mathbb{R}
, it follows that if
\chi_{\alpha}(a)=1
for all
\alpha\in\Sigma
, then
a=\operatorname{Id}
. This is a first-order property and hence also true over
\mathbb{F}
by the transfer principle, so
\psi
is injective. For surjectivity, we construct an explicit preimage of
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\mathbb{F}_{>0})
using the Cartan matrix
B_{ij}=b(\delta_{i},\delta_{j})
for a basis
\Delta=\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
of
\Sigma
, see Section
2.2
. Since
B\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times r}
is non-degenerate, there exists an inverse
B^{-1}\in\mathbb{Q}^{r\times r}
with entries
B_{jk}^{-1}
. Note that rational powers of elements in
A_{\mathbb{F}}
exist and are uniquely defined, as they are over
\mathbb{R}
and this is a first-order property. Now consider

a\coloneqq\prod_{j,k=1}^{r}t_{\delta_{j}}(f(\delta_{k}))^{B_{jk}^{-1}}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}.

For arbitrary
\gamma=\sum_{i=1}^{r}y_{i}\delta_{i}\in L
we have

\displaystyle\chi_{\gamma}\left(a\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\chi_{\delta_{i}}\left(\prod_{j,k=1}^{r}t_{\delta_{j}}(f(\delta_{k}))^{B_{jk}^{-1}}\right)^{y_{i}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j,k=1}^{r}\chi_{\delta_{i}}\left(t_{\delta_{j}}(f(\delta_{k}))\right)^{B_{jk}^{-1}y_{i}}=\prod_{i,k=1}^{r}f(\delta_{k})^{\sum_{j=1}^{r}B_{ij}B_{jk}^{-1}y_{i}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{r}f(\delta_{i})^{y_{i}}=f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}y_{i}\delta_{i}\right)=f(\gamma),

so
\psi
is an isomorphism. Taking
f=\gamma\mapsto\chi_{\gamma}(a)
gives the stated formula for
q_{\delta_{j},\delta_{k}}\coloneq B_{jk}^{-1}
.
∎

We now prove the analogue of Proposition
4.9
in the setting of
\Lambda
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
L:=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)
. The map
\overline{\psi}\colon A_{\Lambda}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\Lambda)
that sends
\xi\in A_{\Lambda}
to
\gamma\mapsto\overline{\chi}_{\gamma}(\xi)
is a group isomorphism.

Proof.

It is clear that
\overline{\psi}
is a well defined group homomorphism. By the isomorphism theorem applied to
f
in the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\mathbb{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\mathbb{F}_{>0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\Lambda)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\psi}}

it suffices to show that
\operatorname{ker}(f)=\left\{a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\colon N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)\in O\right\}
. Indeed
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
satisfies
N_{\mathbb{F}}(a)\in O
if and only if
\chi_{\gamma}(a)=\psi(a)(\gamma)\in O
for all
\gamma\in L
, if and only if
f(a)=0\in\Lambda
.
∎

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
L:=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma)
and
L^{\vee}:=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sigma^{\vee})
. The
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map
\overline{\varphi}\colon L^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\Lambda\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\Lambda)
that sends the basis element
\alpha^{\vee}\otimes\lambda
to
\gamma\mapsto\gamma(\alpha^{\vee})\cdot\lambda
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space isomorphism.

Proof.

Let as before
\Delta=\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}
be a basis of
\Sigma
. It is clear that

\overline{\varphi}\colon\quad\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta_{i}^{\vee}\otimes\lambda_{i}\mapsto\left(\gamma\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{r}\gamma(\delta_{i}^{\vee})\cdot\lambda_{i}\right)

is well defined and
\mathbb{Q}
-linear. We will now use the Cartan matrix
B
of the root system
\Sigma^{\vee}
analogous to the previous proofs to show

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}^{-1}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\Lambda)

\displaystyle\to L^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\Lambda

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}\delta_{j}^{\vee}\otimes f(\delta_{k})

is an inverse of
\overline{\varphi}
. Indeed, for
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\Lambda)
,

\displaystyle[\overline{\varphi}\circ\overline{\varphi}^{-1}(f)]\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}{z_{i}\delta_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[\overline{\varphi}\left(\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}\delta_{j}^{\vee}\otimes f(\delta_{k})\right)\right]\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}{z_{i}\delta_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}B_{j,k}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{r}z_{i}\delta_{i}(\delta_{j}^{\vee})f(\delta_{k})=\sum_{j,k,i=1}^{r}z_{i}B_{ij}B_{jk}^{-1}f(\delta_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,k=1}^{r}z_{i}\operatorname{Id}_{ik}f(\delta_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}z_{i}f(\delta_{i})=f\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}z_{i}\delta_{i}\right)

and for
\lambda_{i}\in\Lambda
,

\displaystyle\overline{\varphi}^{-1}\left(\overline{\varphi}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta_{i}^{\vee}\otimes\lambda_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}\left(\delta_{i}^{\vee}\otimes\overline{\varphi}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta_{i}^{\vee}\otimes\lambda_{i}\right)(\delta_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}\delta_{j}\otimes\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta_{k}(\delta_{i}^{\vee})\lambda_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}B_{ki}\delta_{j}^{\vee}\otimes\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,i=1}^{r}\operatorname{Id}_{ji}\delta_{j}^{\vee}\otimes\lambda_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta_{i}^{\vee}\otimes\lambda_{i}.

which shows that
\overline{\varphi}
is an isomorphism.
∎

Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
together give an isomorphism
f=\overline{\psi}^{-1}\circ\overline{\varphi}\colon\mathbb{A}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{\Lambda}
, where
\mathbb{A}=L^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\Lambda
is the model apartment associated to the coroot system
\Sigma^{\vee}
. We take the full translation group
T=\mathbb{A}
.

Theorem 4.12
.

There is a group isomorphism
f\colon\mathbb{A}\xrightarrow{\sim}A_{\Lambda}
by which
A_{\Lambda}
is given the structure of a model apartment of type
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(\Sigma^{\vee},\Lambda,A_{\Lambda})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(L,\Lambda)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\Lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\varphi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\psi}}

The following allows us to describe walls and halfapartments in terms of the roots.

Proposition 4.13
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{A}
,
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
,
f(x)=[a]
if and only if for all
\alpha\in\Sigma
we have

(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))=b^{\vee}(x,\alpha)=\alpha(x)\in\Lambda,

where
b
is the non-degenerate bilinear form associated to
\Sigma
.

Proof.

Let as before
B
be the Cartan matrix of
\Sigma
. We use Lemma
4.6
to see that
b^{\vee}(\delta_{j}^{\vee},\delta_{\ell})=b(\delta_{\ell},\delta_{j}^{\vee})=B_{\ell j}
. According to Propositions
4.10
and
4.11
,

x=\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}\delta_{j}^{\vee}\otimes\overline{\chi}_{\delta_{k}}(f(x))=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\delta_{j}^{\vee}\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{r}B_{kj}^{-1}\overline{\chi}_{\delta_{k}}(f(x)).

Let
\eta=\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}z_{\ell}\delta_{\ell}
, then

\displaystyle b^{\vee}(x,\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}\overline{\chi}_{\delta_{k}}(f(x))b^{\vee}(\delta_{j}^{\vee},\eta)=\sum_{j,k,\ell=1}^{r}B_{jk}^{-1}z_{\ell}\overline{\chi}_{\delta_{k}}(f(x))b^{\vee}(\delta_{j}^{\vee},\delta_{\ell})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,k,\ell=1}^{r}B_{\ell j}B_{jk}^{-1}z_{\ell}\overline{\chi}_{\delta_{k}}(f(x))=\sum_{\ell,k=1}^{r}\operatorname{Id}_{\ell,k}z_{\ell}\overline{\chi}_{\delta_{k}}(f(x))=\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}z_{\ell}\overline{\chi}_{\delta_{\ell}}(f(x))

\displaystyle=\overline{\chi}_{\sum_{\ell=1}^{r}z_{\ell}\delta_{\ell}}(f(x))=\overline{\chi}_{\eta}(f(x)).

If
f(x)=[a]
for some
a\in\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{F}}
, then
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))=\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}(f(x))=\alpha(x)
for all
\alpha\in\Sigma
. On the other hand,
f(x)
is fully determined by the values
\overline{\psi}(f(x))(\gamma)=\overline{\chi}_{\gamma}(f(x))
for
\gamma\in L
, which are determined by linear combination of the given equations.
∎

Corollary 4.14
.

For all
x,y\in\mathbb{A}
, if
f(x)=[a]
and
f(y)=[b]
for some
a,b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
, then
f(x+y)=[ab]
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.13
, for all
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))=\alpha(x)
and
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(b))=\alpha(y)
, so

(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(ab))=(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a)\chi_{\alpha}(b))=(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))+(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(b))=\alpha(x+y),

so by Proposition
4.13

f(x+y)=[ab]
.
∎

We see from the definition of walls and halfapartments in Section
2.3
that for
\lambda\in\Lambda
and
\alpha\in\Sigma,

\displaystyle M_{\alpha^{\vee},\lambda}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon b^{\vee}(x,\alpha)=\lambda\right\}=\left\{\xi\in A_{\Lambda}\colon\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}(\xi)=\lambda\right\},

\displaystyle H_{\alpha^{\vee},\lambda}^{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon b^{\vee}(x,\alpha)\geq\lambda\right\}=\left\{\xi\in A_{\Lambda}\colon\overline{\chi}_{\alpha}(\xi)\geq\lambda\right\}

under the identification
\mathbb{A}\cong L^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\Lambda\cong A_{\Lambda}
. We will abbreviate
M_{\alpha,\lambda}:=M_{\alpha^{\vee},\lambda}
and
H_{\alpha,\lambda}^{+}:=H_{\alpha^{\vee},\lambda}^{+}
.

We note that
\Sigma^{\vee}
