L=(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}N.

Proof.

Clearly
\supseteq
. We consider the word length of an element
g\in L
. If
g
consists of at most two letters, it is clearly part of
(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}N
, unless
g=u^{\prime}u
for
u^{\prime}\in(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
and
u\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
. In this case, we write
u^{\prime}=u^{\prime\prime}m(u)u^{\prime\prime\prime}
for
u^{\prime\prime},u^{\prime\prime\prime}\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
. Then
\bar{u}:=m(u)u^{\prime\prime\prime}um(u)^{-1}\in(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
by Lemma
5.31
and we have
g=u^{\prime\prime}\bar{u}m(u)\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}N
.

If
g
contains at least three letters, write
g=u_{n}u_{n-1}\ldots u_{2}u_{1}
, where
u_{i}
are elements in
(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}},(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
alternating. Write
u_{2}=u_{2}^{\prime}m(u_{2})u_{2}^{\prime\prime}
with
u_{2}^{\prime},u_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in(U_{-\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}
, where
u_{2}\in(U_{\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}
for
\beta\in\{\pm\alpha\}
. Using Lemma
5.31
again we obtain
\bar{u}:=m(u_{2})u_{2}^{\prime\prime}u_{1}m(u_{2})^{-1}\in(U_{\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}
, and

g=u_{n}\ldots u_{4}(u_{3}u_{2}^{\prime})\bar{u}m(u)
, so
g\in hN
for some
h\in L
with a smaller word length. Applying induction gives the result.
∎

The following description of the stabilizer of a subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
under the action of the rank 1 subgroup
L
will not be used later, but is included for completeness.

Proposition 5.39
.

Let
\alpha\in\Sigma
,
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
non-empty finite,
L=\langle(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}},(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\rangle
,
N=\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cap L
and
N_{\Omega}=\{g\in N\colon n.p=p\text{ for all }p\in\Omega\}
. Then

\operatorname{Stab}_{L}(\Omega)=\langle U_{\alpha,\Omega},U_{-\alpha,\Omega},N_{\Omega}\rangle=U_{\alpha,\Omega}U_{-\alpha,\Omega}N_{\Omega}.

Proof.

The inclusions
\supseteq
are clear. Let

\ell=\min_{a.o\in\Omega}\{(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))\}\quad\text{and}\quad\ell^{\prime}=\min_{a.o\in\Omega}\{(-v)(\chi_{-\alpha}(a))\}.

Then
\ell\leq-\ell^{\prime}
since
\Omega\neq\emptyset
,
U_{\alpha,\ell}=U_{\alpha,\Omega}
and
U_{-\alpha,\ell^{\prime}}=U_{-\alpha,\Omega}
. Now if
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{L}(\Omega)
, then there are
u\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}},u^{\prime}\in(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}},n\in N
such that
g.p=uu^{\prime}n.p=p
for all
p\in\Omega
, by Lemma
5.38
. We distinguish three cases for
\varphi_{\alpha}(u)
. If
\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\leq\ell
, then
u\in U_{\alpha,\ell}
and
u^{\prime}n.p=u^{-1}.p=p
for all
p\in\Omega
, so
u^{\prime}\in U_{-\alpha,\ell^{\prime}}
and
n\in N_{\Omega}
by Proposition
5.7
, so
g\in U_{\alpha,\ell}U_{-\alpha,\ell^{\prime}}N_{\Omega}
.

If
\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\geq-\ell^{\prime}
, then we write
u=u^{\prime\prime}m(u)u^{\prime\prime\prime}
for some
u^{\prime\prime},u^{\prime\prime\prime}\in(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
with
\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime\prime})=\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime\prime\prime})=-\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\leq\ell^{\prime}
. Abbreviating
\bar{u}:=m(u)u^{\prime\prime\prime}u^{\prime}m(u)^{-1}\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
we obtain

g=uu^{\prime}n=u^{\prime\prime}\bar{u}m(u)n\in U_{-\alpha,\ell^{\prime}}\cdot(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cdot N
by Lemma
5.31
. Then
\bar{u}m(u)n.p=p
for all
p\in\Omega
, whence
\varphi_{\alpha}(\bar{u})\leq\ell
by Proposition
5.7
and then
m(u)n.p=p
for all
p\in\Omega
, so
m(u)n\in N_{\Omega}
. Then
g\in U_{\alpha,\ell}U_{-\alpha,\ell^{\prime}}N_{\Omega}
.

We claim that the final case
\ell<\varphi_{\alpha}(u)<-\ell^{\prime}
cannot happen. For this we first notice that for
p\in\Omega
we have
u.p=p
if and only if
u^{\prime}n.p=p
, if and only if
u^{\prime}.p=p
. Now let
a_{1},a_{2}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a_{1}))=\ell
and
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a_{2}))=-\ell^{\prime}
. Now
u.a_{1}.o\neq a_{1}.o
, so
u^{\prime}.a_{1}.o\neq a_{1}.o
, so
\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime})>-\ell
, but
u.a_{2}.o=a_{2}.o
, so
u^{\prime}.a_{2}.o=a_{2}.o
, so
\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime})\leq\ell^{\prime}
. But this is not possible, since then
-\ell\leq\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime})\leq\ell^{\prime}
contradicting
\ell<\ell^{\prime}
.
We have shown that
\operatorname{Stab}_{L}(\Omega)\subseteq U_{\alpha,\ell}U_{-\alpha,\ell^{\prime}}N_{\Omega}
.
∎

5.7.
Bruhat-Tits theory in higher rank

In this section, we continue to assume that
\Sigma
is a reduced root system. Let
>
be an order on
\Sigma
and
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
. Now for any subset
\Theta\subseteq\Sigma_{>0}
closed under addition, let

U_{\Theta,\Omega}:=\left\{u\in\exp\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Theta}(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\right)\colon u.p=p\text{ for all }p\in\Omega\right\}

and in particular

U_{\Omega}^{+}:=U_{\Sigma_{>0},\Omega}=\left\{u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}\colon u.p=p\text{ for all }p\in\Omega\right\}

for
\Theta=\Sigma_{>0}
. Note that
U_{\emptyset}^{+}=U_{\mathbb{F}}
. Analogously we define

U_{\Omega}^{-}:=\left\{u\in\exp\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha<0}(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\right)\colon u.p=p\text{ for all }p\in\Omega\right\}.

Let

N_{\Omega}:=\left\{n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\colon n.p=p\text{ for all }p\in\Omega\right\}.

When
\Omega=\{p\}
consists of a single point, we abbreviate the notation by omitting the brackets such as in
U_{p}^{+}:=U_{\{p\}}^{+}
,
U_{p}^{-}:=U_{\{p\}}^{-}
and
N_{p}:=N_{\{p\}}
. The goal of this subsection is to prove in Theorem
5.45
that if
\Omega
is finite, then the pointwise stabilizer of
\Omega
satisfies

\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)=U_{\Omega}^{+}U_{\Omega}^{-}N_{\Omega}.

Proposition 5.40
.

For any subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
and subset
\Theta\subseteq\Sigma_{>0}
closed under addition,

U_{\Theta,\Omega}=\left\langle u\in U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Theta\right\rangle

and in particular,

U_{\Omega}^{+}=\langle u\in U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha>0\rangle.

More precisely, if
\Sigma_{>0}=\left\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\right\}
with
\alpha_{1}<\ldots<\alpha_{r}
, then the product map

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}}U_{\alpha,\Omega}

\displaystyle\to U_{\Omega}^{+}

\displaystyle(u_{\alpha_{1}},\ldots,u_{\alpha_{r}})

\displaystyle\mapsto u_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots u_{\alpha_{r}}

is a bijection.

Proof.

For any
\Theta=\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\}\subseteq\Sigma_{>0}
closed under addition with
\alpha_{1}>\ldots>\alpha_{k}
, the image of
\prod_{\alpha\in\Theta}U_{\alpha,\Omega}
under the product map is contained in
U_{\Theta,\Omega}
. On the other hand, if we start with
u\in U_{\Theta,\Omega}
, we can apply
[
App25
, Lemma
]
to obtain a unique
(u_{1},\cdots,u_{k})\in(U_{\alpha_{1}})\times\ldots(U_{\alpha_{k}})_{\mathbb{F}}
with
u=u_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot u_{k}
. A point
a.o\in\Omega
is fixed by
u
if and only if
a^{-1}ua\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
, so

a^{-1}u_{1}a\cdot\ldots\cdot a^{-1}u_{k}a\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O).

We can apply Lemma
5.9
repeatedly to obtain
a^{-1}u_{1}a\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
, …,
a^{-1}u_{k}a\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
for all
a.o\in\Omega
. This exactly means that
u_{i}\in U_{\alpha,\Omega}
. This shows that the product map is surjective. Note that the order in the statement of the Proposition is the inverse of the one used in the proof, but one follows from the other by applying the inverse.
∎

We notice that the previous propositions hold for any chosen order on
\Sigma
. We may in particular invert the order to obtain

\displaystyle U_{\Omega}^{-}=\langle u\in U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha<0\rangle

from Proposition
5.40
.
We now define the groups

P_{\Omega}:=\langle U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma\rangle\quad\text{ and }\quad\hat{P}_{\Omega}:=\langle N_{\Omega},U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma\rangle

generated by all elements of
U_{\alpha,\Omega}
, not just those with positive
\alpha
. The following is a generalization of Lemma
5.36
to higher rank.

Proposition 5.41
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
finite. Then
P_{\Omega}
and
\hat{P}_{\Omega}
decompose as

\displaystyle P_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=U_{\Omega}^{-}\cdot U_{\Omega}^{+}\cdot(\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cap P_{\Omega})

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=U_{\Omega}^{-}\cdot U_{\Omega}^{+}\cdot N_{\Omega}.

Proof.

The inclusions
\supseteq
are clear. Recall that the set
\Sigma_{>0}
is determined by a basis
\Delta\subseteq\Sigma
, or equivalently a chamber. After choosing a chamber, an ordering
>
of
\Sigma
can be obtained by choosing an order
\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\ldots<\alpha_{r}
on
\Delta=\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{r}\}
; the ordering on
\Sigma
is then the lexicographical ordering
[
Bou08
, VI.1.6, p. 174-175]
.

For the
\subseteq
direction of the description of
P_{\Omega}
, we will first show that

U_{\Omega}^{-}\cdot U_{\Omega}^{+}\cdot(\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cap P_{\Omega})

is independent of the chamber defining
\Sigma_{>0}
.
Let
<_{1},<_{2}
be two orderings on
\Sigma
whose chambers
C_{1},C_{2}
are related by a reflection determined by a simple root
\alpha\in\Delta_{1}\subseteq\Sigma
, so
\Delta_{2}=r_{\alpha}(\Delta_{1})
. We may then assume that
<_{1}
and
<_{2}
are determined by lexicographical orderings such that
0<_{1}\alpha<_{1}\beta
for all
\beta\in\Delta_{1}\setminus\{\alpha\}
and
0<_{2}-\alpha<_{2}\beta
for all
\beta\in\Delta_{2}\setminus\{\alpha\}
. We notice that then
\Sigma_{>_{1}0}\setminus\{\alpha\}=\Sigma_{>_{2}0}\setminus\{-\alpha\}
, since for
\beta\in\Sigma_{>_{1}0}\setminus\{\alpha\}
there are
\lambda_{\delta}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, at least one of which is strictly positive for some
\delta\in\Delta_{1}\setminus\{\alpha\}
, such that

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle=\sum_{\delta\in\Delta_{1}}\lambda_{\delta}\delta=\sum_{\delta\in\Delta_{1}}\lambda_{\delta}\left(r_{\alpha}(\delta)-2\frac{\langle\delta,\alpha\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}r_{\alpha}(\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\delta\in\Delta_{1}\setminus\{\alpha\}}\lambda_{\delta}r_{\alpha}(\delta)+\left(\sum_{\delta\in\Delta_{1}\setminus\{\alpha\}}-2\frac{\langle\delta,\alpha\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\lambda_{\delta}-\lambda_{\alpha}\right)r_{\alpha}(\alpha)

\displaystyle\in\sum_{\delta\in\Delta_{1}\setminus\{\alpha\}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}r_{\alpha}(\delta)+\mathbb{Z}r_{\alpha}(\alpha)=\sum_{\delta\in\Delta_{2}\setminus\{-\alpha\}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\delta+\mathbb{Z}(-\alpha),

where we first used that
\langle\delta,\alpha\rangle
is nonpositive for all
\delta\in\Delta_{1}\setminus\{\alpha\}
. Bases of a root system have the property that any element of the root system written in that basis has either all non-negative or all non-positive coefficients. Since there is a strictly positive coefficient for the element
\beta
written in the basis
\Delta_{2}
as above, all coefficients have to be non-negative, so
\beta\in\Sigma_{>_{2}0}\setminus\{-\alpha\}
. It now suffices to show that

U_{\Omega}^{-}\cdot U_{\Omega}^{+}\cdot(\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cap P_{\Omega})

is the same for
<_{1}
and
<_{2}
to deduce that it is independent of the chamber used to define
\Sigma_{>0}
, since the reflections determined by the simple roots
\Delta
generate the spherical Weyl group which acts transitively on the chambers.

We abbreviate
Y:=(\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cap P_{\Omega})
. We use Proposition
5.40
repeatedly to obtain

\displaystyle U_{\Sigma_{<_{1}0},\Omega}\cdot U_{\Sigma_{>_{1}0},\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{\beta<_{1}0}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot\prod_{\beta>_{1}0}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta<_{1}0\\
-\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{-\alpha,\Omega}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta>_{1}0\\
\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{\alpha,\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta<_{1}0\\
-\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{\Sigma_{>_{2}0},\Omega}\cdot U_{\alpha,\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta<_{1}0\\
-\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta>_{1}0\\
\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{-\alpha,\Omega}\cdot U_{\alpha,\Omega}\cdot Y

at which point we invoke Lemma
5.36
to continue

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta<_{1}0\\
-\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta>_{1}0\\
\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{\alpha,\Omega}\cdot U_{-\alpha,\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta<_{1}0\\
-\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot\prod_{\beta>_{1}0}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{-\alpha,\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta<_{1}0\\
-\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{\alpha,\Omega}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta>_{1}0\\
\alpha\neq\beta\end{subarray}}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot U_{-\alpha,\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=\prod_{\beta<_{2}0}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot\prod_{\beta>_{2}0}U_{\beta,\Omega}\cdot Y

\displaystyle=U_{\Sigma_{<_{2}0},\Omega}\cdot U_{\Sigma_{>_{2}0},\Omega}\cdot Y.

Now that we have shown that it is independent of the chamber defining the order on
\Sigma
, we show the direction
\subseteq
by induction on word length. The base case is clear. Now let
u_{1}\in U_{\alpha,\Omega}
for some
\alpha\in\Sigma
and let
u\in P_{\Omega}
. We may choose the order on
\Sigma
such that
u_{1}\in U_{\Omega}^{-}
. Then by the induction assumption, we have
u\in U_{\Omega}^{-}\cdot U_{\Omega}^{+}\cdot Y
and hence also
u_{1}u\in U_{\Omega}^{-}\cdot U_{\Omega}^{+}\cdot Y
.

It remains to show
\hat{P}\subseteq U_{\Omega}^{-}U_{\Omega}^{+}N_{\Omega}
. We use the fact that for every
n\in N_{\Omega}
we have
nU_{\alpha,\Omega}n^{-1}=U_{[n](\alpha),n.\Omega}U_{[n](\alpha),\Omega}
where
[n]
is the representative of the spherical Weyl group corresponding to
n
. This can be used to show

\hat{P}_{\Omega}=\langle N_{\Omega},U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma\rangle=\langle U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma\rangle\cdot N_{\Omega}=P_{\Omega}N_{\Omega}=U_{\Omega}^{-}U_{\Omega}^{+}N_{\Omega},

making use of the description of
P_{\Omega}
.
∎

Next, we will obtain a ’mixed Iwasawa’-decomposition for the group
G_{\mathbb{F}}
, upgrading the result of Proposition
5.37
in rank 1.

Theorem 5.42
.

For every order on the root system
\Sigma
,

G_{\mathbb{F}}=U^{+}\cdot\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cdot\hat{P}_{o}
.

Proof.

We abbreviate
\tilde{G}:=U^{+}\cdot\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cdot\hat{P}_{o}
. It suffices to show that
g\tilde{G}\subseteq\tilde{G}
for every
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
, since then
G_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq G_{\mathbb{F}}\tilde{G}\subseteq\tilde{G}\subseteq G_{\mathbb{F}}
. By
[
App25
, Theorem
]
, and the fact that non-trivial elements of the spherical Weyl group
W_{s}
of the form
m(u)
can be obtained from elements in
U^{+}
and
U^{-}
,
G_{\mathbb{F}}=\langle U^{+},U^{-},T_{\mathbb{F}}\rangle
, where

\displaystyle U^{+}

\displaystyle=\langle(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\alpha>0\rangle

\displaystyle U^{-}

\displaystyle=\langle(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\alpha<0\rangle

\displaystyle T_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Cen}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})

as earlier. Since
U^{+}\tilde{G}\subseteq\tilde{G}
and
T_{\mathbb{F}}\tilde{G}\subseteq\tilde{G}
, it suffices to show
U^{-}\tilde{G}\subseteq\tilde{G}
. Since equality holds in
[(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}},(\mathfrak{g}_{\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}]=(\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha+\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}
,

U^{-}=\langle(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\alpha\in\Delta\rangle

for the basis
\Delta\subseteq\Sigma
associated to the chosen order, compare
[
Bor91
, Remark 14.5(2)]
. Therefore it suffices to show
(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\tilde{G}\subseteq\tilde{G}
for all
\alpha\in\Delta
to show the theorem.

Now let
\alpha\in\Delta
and consider the complete lexicographical order on
\Sigma
such that
\alpha<\delta
for all
\delta\in\Delta
. Let
U_{\alpha}^{\prime}:=\langle(U_{\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}\colon\beta>0,\beta\neq\alpha\rangle\subseteq U^{+}
. Then by
[
App25
, Lemma
]
,
U^{+}=(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cdot U_{\alpha}^{\prime}=U_{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
. We note that when we instead consider the order with basis
r_{\alpha}(\Delta)
,
-\alpha
is the smallest positive element as in the proof of Proposition
5.40
and then
[
App25
, Lemma
]
gives
(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cdot U_{\alpha}^{\prime}=U_{\alpha}^{\prime}\cdot(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
. We use Proposition
5.37
to show

\displaystyle(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\tilde{G}

\displaystyle=(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}U_{\alpha}^{\prime}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle=U_{\alpha}^{\prime}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle\subseteq U_{\alpha}^{\prime}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\cap(L_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}})L_{o}\cdot\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle\subseteq U^{+}T_{\mathbb{F}}L_{o}\cdot\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}\ \amalg\ U^{+}T_{\mathbb{F}}mL_{o}\cdot\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o},

where
L_{o}=\langle U_{\alpha,o},U_{-\alpha,o}\rangle
and
m\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
represents the reflection
r_{\alpha}
in the spherical Weyl group
W_{s}
. By Lemma
5.36
,
L_{o}\subseteq U_{\alpha,o}U_{-\alpha,o}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
and
L_{o}\subseteq U_{-\alpha,o}U_{\alpha,o}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
. Then

\displaystyle(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\tilde{G}

\displaystyle\subseteq U^{+}T_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}\ \amalg\ U^{+}T_{\mathbb{F}}m(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle=U^{+}T_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle=U^{+}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}T_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle=U^{+}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

where we used
m(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}m^{-1}=(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
and
m(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}m^{-1}=(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
. We claim that for every
u^{\prime}\in(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
and
n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
,
u^{\prime}n\in\tilde{G}
. To see this, consider
\beta=[n^{-1}](\alpha)
and
v:=n^{-1}u^{\prime}n\in(U_{[n^{-1}](-\alpha)})_{\mathbb{F}}=(U_{-\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}
. Then we can apply the rank one mixed Iwasawa decomposition, Proposition
5.37
, for
\beta
to obtain

\displaystyle u^{\prime}n=nv

\displaystyle\in n(L_{\pm\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq n(U_{\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle=n(U_{\beta})_{\mathbb{F}}n^{-1}n\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}

\displaystyle=(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}\subseteq\tilde{G}.

Assuming the claim,
(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\tilde{G}\subseteq U^{+}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}\subseteq U^{+}\tilde{G}\hat{P}_{o}=\tilde{G}
completes the proof.
∎

The mixed Iwasawa decomposition can be used to show that
\hat{P}_{o}=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(o)
. In fact we will eventually show that
\hat{P}_{\Omega}
is the whole pointwise stabilizer of
\Omega
in Theorem
5.49
.

Proposition 5.43
.

The stabilizer of a single point
p\in\mathbb{A}
satisfies

\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(p)=\hat{P}_{p}=\langle N_{p},U_{\alpha,p}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma\rangle=U_{p}^{-}U_{p}^{+}N_{p}.

Proof.

The two expressions on the right coincide with
\hat{P}_{p}
, see Proposition
5.41
. The inclusion
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(p)\supseteq\hat{P}_{p}
is clear. For the other direction, we first consider
p=o
and use the mixed Iwasawa decomposition, Theorem
5.42
,
G_{\mathbb{F}}=U^{+}\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\hat{P}_{o}
. For
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(o)
, let
u\in U^{+},n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
and
p\in\hat{P}_{o}
with
g=unp
. Then
o=g.o=unp.o=un.o=n.o
by Proposition
5.7
. Thus
n\in N_{o}
and hence also
u\in U_{o}^{+}=\langle U_{\alpha,o}\colon\alpha>0\rangle
.

For general
p=a.o\in\mathbb{A}
and elements
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
,
g.p=p
if and only if
a^{-1}ga.o=o
. Use Proposition
5.41
to write
a^{-1}ga=uu^{\prime}n
as a product of elements
u\in U^{+}_{o},u^{\prime}\in U_{o}^{-}
and
n\in N_{o}
. We note that
aua^{-1}\in U_{p}^{+},au^{\prime}a^{-1}\in U_{p}^{-}
and
ana^{-1}\in N_{p}
, whence
g\in\hat{P}_{p}
.
∎

We state a Lemma that allows us to prove that
\hat{P}_{\Omega}
is the pointwise stabilizer of any finite subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
.

Lemma 5.44
.

For
p,q\in\mathbb{A}
there is an order on
\Sigma
such that
U_{q}^{+}\subseteq U_{p}^{+}
.

Proof.

If
q=o
and
p
lies in the fundamental Weyl chamber
C_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{A}
, then we can take the standard order
>
associated to
C_{0}
. Elements
g\in U_{q}^{+}=U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
stabilize all of
C_{0}
pointwise, see Theorem
5.17
, so
U_{q}^{+}\subseteq U_{p}^{+}
.

In general, there is an element
n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
with
n.q=0
and
n.p\in C_{0}
(
n
is a translation by
-q
followed by a representative of a unique element in the spherical Weyl group). Then by the above
U_{n.q}^{+}\subseteq U_{n.p}^{+}
with respect to the standard order associated to
C_{0}
. If
>_{2}
and
+_{2}
denote the order with positive roots
\Sigma_{>_{2}0}=\left\{\alpha\in\Sigma\colon[n^{-1}](\alpha)>0\right\}
, then
U_{n.p}^{+}=n^{-1}U_{p}^{+_{2}}n
, so

\displaystyle U_{q}^{+_{2}}

\displaystyle=nU_{n.q}^{+}n^{-1}\subseteq nU_{n.p}^{+}n^{-1}=U_{p}^{+_{2}}.\qed

Theorem 5.45
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
be a finite subset. Then the pointwise stabilizer of
\Omega
satisfies

\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)=\hat{P}_{\Omega}=\langle N_{\Omega},U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha\in\Sigma\rangle=U_{\Omega}^{+}U_{\Omega}^{-}N_{\Omega}.

Proof.

We claim that

\hat{P}_{\Omega\cup\{p\}}=\hat{P}_{\Omega}\cap\hat{P}_{p}

for all non-empty, finite
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
and
p\in\mathbb{A}
. The inclusion
\subseteq
is clear. Now let
g\in\hat{P}_{\Omega}\cap\hat{P}_{p}
. For some
q\in\Omega
, choose an ordering of
\Sigma
such that
U_{q}^{+}\subseteq U_{p}^{+}
by Lemma
5.44
.
Now use Proposition
5.41
to write
g=nu^{\prime}u
with
n\in N_{\Omega}
,
u^{\prime}\in U_{\Omega}^{-}
,
u\in U_{\Omega}^{+}
. We have
u\in U_{\Omega}^{+}\subseteq U_{q}^{+}\subseteq U_{p}^{+}
, so
gu^{-1}=nu^{\prime}\in\hat{P}_{p}
. Since now
u^{\prime}.p=n^{-1}.p
, we have
u^{\prime}.p=p
by Proposition
5.7
. This means that all three elements
u,u^{\prime}
and
n
fix
\Omega\cup\{p\}
, so
g\in N_{\Omega\cup\{p\}}U_{\Omega\cup\{p\}}^{-}U_{\Omega\cup\{p\}}^{+}=\hat{P}_{\Omega\cup\{p\}}
.

We now show
\operatorname{Stab}(\Omega)=\hat{P}_{\Omega}
by induction over the size of
\Omega
. If
|\Omega|=1
, this is Proposition
5.43
. Now assume the statement holds for
\Omega
. Given any
p\in\mathbb{A}
, we have

\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega\cup\{p\})=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)\cap\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(p)=\hat{P}_{\Omega}\cap\hat{P}_{p}=\hat{P}_{\Omega\cup\{p\}},

where we used the induction assumption and the claim.
∎

5.8.
Axiom (A2)

In this section we assume
\Sigma
to be reduced. We are now equipped to prove axiom (A2). We first show a special case.

Proposition 5.46
.

Let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\Omega=g^{-1}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}
. Then there exists an element
w\in W_{a}
such that for all
p\in\Omega
,
g.p=w(p)
.

Proof.

We may assume that
\Omega\neq\emptyset
. For subsets
Y\subseteq\Omega
we consider

N_{g,Y}:=\left\{n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\colon g.p=n.p\text{ for all }p\in Y\right\},

so that our goal is to prove that
N_{g,\Omega}\neq\emptyset
.
If
Y=\{p\}
is a singleton, then
N_{g,\left\{p\right\}}
is non-empty, since if
p=a.o
and
g.p=b.o
for
a,b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
, then
ba^{-1}\in N_{\{p\}}
. We will now show by induction on the size of
Y
, that
N_{g,Y}\neq\emptyset
for all
finite
sets
Y\subseteq\Omega
.

Let
Y\subseteq\Omega
finite and
p\in\Omega
. Assume that there are
n_{Y}\in N_{g,Y}
and
n_{p}\in N_{g,\{p\}}
, then
g^{-1}n_{Y}
and
g^{-1}n_{p}
lie in the pointwise stabilizers
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(Y)
and
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\{p\})
. We may choose an order on
\Sigma
such that
U_{Y}^{+}\subseteq U_{\{p\}}^{+}
, by making sure that the defining chamber for the order based at some point in
Y
contains
p
, see Lemma
5.44
.
Then, by Theorem
5.45
,

\displaystyle n_{Y}^{-1}n_{p}

\displaystyle\in N_{Y}U_{Y}^{-}U_{Y}^{+}U_{\{p\}}^{+}U_{\{p\}}^{-}N_{\{p\}}=N_{Y}U_{Y}^{-}U_{\{p\}}^{+}U_{\{p\}}^{-}N_{\{p\}}

\displaystyle=N_{Y}U_{Y}^{-}U_{\{p\}}^{-}U_{\{p\}}^{+}N_{\{p\}}\subseteq N_{Y}U^{-}U^{+}N_{\{p\}}

Let
n_{Y}^{\prime}\in N_{Y},n_{p}^{\prime}\in N_{\{p\}}
such that
(n_{Y}^{\prime})^{-1}n_{Y}^{-1}n_{p}n_{p}^{\prime}\in U^{-}U^{+}\cap\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
. Since every element of
U^{+}
stabilizes some affine chamber pointwise, so does every element of
U^{-}U^{+}\cap\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
, by Proposition
5.7
. The element of the affine Weyl group represented by
(n_{Y}^{\prime})^{-1}n_{Y}^{-1}n_{p}n_{p}^{\prime}
thus acts trivially on some affine chamber, hence acts trivially on all of
\mathbb{A}
.
Therefore
[n_{Y}n_{Y}^{\prime}]=[n_{p}n_{p}^{\prime}]\in W_{a}
and
n_{Y}n_{Y}^{\prime}\in N_{g,Y}\cap N_{g,\{p\}}=N_{g,Y\cup\{p\}}
, concluding the induction.

Recall that the affine Weyl group
W_{a}=W_{s}\ltimes\mathbb{A}
is isomorphic to the quotient
\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})/\operatorname{Cen}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
. Let
\pi\colon\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})\to W_{s}
be the induced map to the finite group
W_{s}
. Let
Y_{0}\subseteq\Omega
be a finite subset, so that
|\pi(N_{g,Y_{0}})|
is minimal. For any
p\in\Omega
,

N_{g,Y_{0}\cup\{p\}}=N_{g,Y_{0}}\cap N_{g,\{p\}}\subseteq N_{g,Y_{0}}

and thus by minimality
\pi(N_{g,Y_{0}\cup\{p\}})=\pi(N_{g,Y_{0}})
. We claim that in fact
N_{g,Y_{0}\cup\{p\}}=N_{g,Y_{0}}
for every
p\in\Omega
.
Pick
n_{0}\in N_{g,Y_{0}}
.
We decompose
w:=[n_{0}]=(t_{a},\pi(n_{0}))\in W_{a}=\mathbb{A}\ltimes W_{s}
, so that
t_{a}
is translation given by multiplication of some
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
. For any
p\in\Omega
, there exists
n^{\prime}\in N_{g,Y_{0}\cup\{p\}}
such that
\pi(n_{0})=\pi(n^{\prime})
and we decompose similarly
w^{\prime}:=[n^{\prime}]=(t_{a^{\prime}},\pi(n^{\prime}))\in W_{a}
. Acting on some
q\in Y_{0}
, we have

a.(\pi(n_{0})(q))=n_{0}.q=g.q=n^{\prime}.q=a^{\prime}.(\pi(n^{\prime})(q))=a^{\prime}.(\pi(n_{0})(q)),

which implies
t_{a}=t_{a^{\prime}}
and thus
w=(t_{a},\pi(n_{0}))=(t_{a}^{\prime},\pi(n^{\prime}))=w^{\prime}
. For any
p\in\Omega
we thus have

n_{0}.p=w(p)=w^{\prime}(p)=n^{\prime}.p=g.p,

so
n_{0}\in N_{g,Y_{0}\cup\{p\}}
as required. Finally

N_{g,\Omega}=\bigcap_{p\in\Omega}N_{g,Y_{0}\cup\{p\}}=N_{g,Y_{0}}\neq\emptyset,

so taking any
n\in N_{g,Y_{0}}
provides the required element
w=[n]\in W_{a}
with
w(p)=g.p
for all
p\in\Omega
.
∎

From the above proof, we can also extract the following Lemma, by taking
g=\operatorname{Id}
and noting that then
N_{\Omega}=N_{g,\Omega}=N_{g,Y_{0}}=N_{Y_{0}}
.

Lemma 5.47
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
. There exists a finite subset
Y_{0}\subseteq\Omega
such that
N_{Y_{0}}=N_{\Omega}
.

To be able to prove the
W_{a}
-convexity part of axiom (A2), we want to describe the stabilizer of a (possibly infinite) subset
\Omega
as in Theorem
5.45
. For this we first need a Lemma.

Lemma 5.48
.

Let
u^{+}\in U^{+}
,
u^{-}\in U^{-}
and
p\in\mathbb{A}
. If
u^{+}.p=u^{-}.p
, then
u^{+}.p=p=u^{-}.p
.

Proof.

We start by noting that if
a\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
is an upper triangular matrix and
b\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
is a lower triangular matrix, both with ones on the diagonal such that
ab\in O^{n\times n}
, then
a,b\in O^{n\times n}
, as can be checked by matrix calculations.

We will now prove the Lemma in the case that
p=o\in\mathcal{B}
. We know that
u^{+}.p=u^{-}.p
which is equivalent to
(u^{+})^{-1}u^{-}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
. The adjoint map
\operatorname{Ad}\colon G_{\mathbb{F}}\to\operatorname{GL}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{F}})
is a
\mathbb{K}
-morphism that sends elements of
U^{+}
to upper triangular matrices and elements of
U^{-}
to lower triangular matrices, see
[
App25
, Lemma
]
. Moreover, since
\operatorname{Ad}(g)X=gXg^{-1}
for
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{F}}
, we can use Lemma
5.5
on a basis to see that
\operatorname{Ad}(g)
is defined by polynomials and if
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
, then these polynomials have coefficients in
O
. Thus
\operatorname{Ad}(G_{\mathbb{F}}(O))\subseteq\operatorname{Ad}(G_{\mathbb{F}})(O)
and
\operatorname{Ad}((u^{+})^{-1})\operatorname{Ad}(u^{-})\in O^{n\times n}
and by the preceding remark,
\operatorname{Ad}(u^{+})\in O^{n\times n}
and
\operatorname{Ad}(u^{-})\in O^{n\times n}
.

While
\operatorname{Ad}\colon G_{\mathbb{F}}\to\operatorname{GL}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{F}})
may have a nontrivial finite kernel, its restriction
\operatorname{Ad}|_{U^{+}}\colon U^{+}\to\operatorname{GL}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{F}})
is an isomorphism onto its image, since the exponential map gives an isomorphism
U^{+}\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha>0}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
and since
\operatorname{ad}
restricted to the Lie algebra of
U^{+}
is an isomorphism to its image defined over
\mathbb{K}
. Then, the inverse map
\operatorname{Ad}(U^{+})\to U^{+}
is also defined by polynomials with coefficients in
\mathbb{K}
, so
u^{+}\in O^{n\times n}
. Similarly
u^{-}\in O^{n\times n}
. This means that
u^{+}.o=o
and
u^{-}.o=o
as required.

If now
p=a.o
for some
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
, then
(u^{+})^{-1}u^{-}.p=p
if and only if

a^{-1}(u^{+})^{-1}aa^{-1}u^{-}a\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O).

By the above argument
a^{-1}u^{+}a
,
a^{-1}u^{-}a\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
and thus
u^{+}.p=p
and
u^{-}.p=p
.
∎

We upgrade the description of
\hat{P}_{\Omega}
as the pointwise stabilizer of a finite subset in Theorem
5.45
, to arbitrary subsets
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
.

Theorem 5.49
.

The pointwise stabilizer of any subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{A}
satisfies

\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)=\hat{P}_{\Omega}=U_{\Omega}^{+}U_{\Omega}^{-}N_{\Omega}.

Proof.

The inclusions
\supseteq
are clear. We apply Lemma
5.47
to obtain a finite subset
Y_{0}\subseteq\Omega
such that
N_{Y_{0}}=N_{\Omega}
. Then
\operatorname{Stab}(\Omega)\subseteq\operatorname{Stab}(Y_{0})=U_{Y_{0}}^{+}U_{Y_{0}}^{-}N_{Y_{0}}
by Theorem
5.45
. Let
g\in\operatorname{Stab}(\Omega)
and
u^{+}\in U_{Y_{0}}^{+},u^{-}\in U_{Y_{0}}^{-},n\in N_{Y_{0}}=N_{\Omega}
with
g=u^{+}u^{-}n
. Then
u^{+}u^{-}=gn^{-1}\in\operatorname{Stab}(\Omega)
. Thus,
(u^{+}).p=u^{-}.p
for all
p\in\Omega
and by Lemma
5.48
,
u^{+}.p=p=u^{-}.p
, in particular
u^{+}\in U_{\Omega}^{+}
and
u^{-}\in U_{\Omega}^{-}
. We now know

\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)\subseteq U_{\Omega}^{+}U_{\Omega}^{-}N_{\Omega}\subseteq\hat{P}_{\Omega}\subseteq\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\Omega)

concluding the proof.
∎

We now prove a special case of
W_{a}
-convexity.

Proposition 5.50
.

Let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
. Then
\Omega=g^{-1}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}
is a finite intersection of affine half-apartments.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.46
, we know that there is an
n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
such that
g^{-1}n\in\operatorname{Stab}(\Omega)
pointwise and by Theorem
5.49

g^{-1}n\in U_{\Omega}^{+}U_{\Omega}^{-}N_{\Omega}
. So let
u^{+}\in U_{\Omega}^{+},u^{-}\in U_{\Omega}^{-}
and
n^{\prime}\in N_{\Omega}
with
g^{-1}n=u^{+}u^{-}n^{\prime}
. We note that for
\tilde{n}:=n(n^{\prime})^{-1}
we have
g^{-1}\tilde{n}=u^{+}u^{-}
.

Recall that the affine half-space given by
\alpha\in\Sigma
and
k\in\Lambda
is

H_{\alpha,k}^{+}=\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))\geq k\right\}.

In Proposition
5.10
, we showed that for
u^{+}
there are
k_{\alpha}\in\Lambda\cup\{-\infty\}
for every
\alpha>0
such that

\left\{p\in\mathbb{A}\colon u^{+}.p\in\mathbb{A}\right\}=\bigcap_{\alpha>0}H_{\alpha,k_{\alpha}}^{+}.

where we take the convention that
H_{\alpha,-\infty}^{+}=\mathbb{A}
. By changing the order on
\Sigma
, we similarly obtain for
u^{-}
some
k_{\alpha}\in\Lambda\cup\{-\infty\}
for
\alpha<0
such that

\left\{p\in\mathbb{A}\colon u^{-}.p\in\mathbb{A}\right\}=\bigcap_{\alpha<0}H_{\alpha,k_{\alpha}}^{+}.

We now show that

\Omega=\bigcap_{\alpha\in\Sigma}H_{\alpha,k_{\alpha}}^{+}.

By Lemma
5.48
, for any
p\in\Omega
we have
u^{+}.p=p
and
u^{-}.p=p
, hence
p\in\bigcap H_{\alpha,k_{\alpha}}^{+}
. If on the other hand
p\in\bigcap H_{\alpha,k_{\alpha}}^{+}
, then
u^{+}u^{-}.p=u^{+}.p=p
, in particular
p\in u^{+}u^{-}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}=g^{-1}\tilde{n}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}=g^{-1}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}=\Omega
. This concludes the proof that
\Omega
is a finite intersection of half-spaces.
∎

We now put together Propositions
5.46
and
5.50
to prove axiom (A2).

Theorem 5.51
.

Axiom (A2) holds:

(A2)

For every
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathscr{F}
, the set
B:=f^{-1}(f(\mathbb{A})\cap f^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}))\subseteq\mathbb{A}
is a finite intersection of affine half-apartments and there is
w\in W_{a}
such that
f|_{B}=f^{\prime}\circ w|_{B}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\mathscr{F}
, there are
h,h^{\prime}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
f=h.f_{0}
and
f^{\prime}=h^{\prime}.f_{0}
, where
f_{0}\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is the inclusion. Then for
g:=(h^{\prime})^{-1}h
we have
B=g^{-1}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}
since

f(g^{-1}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A})=h.(g^{-1}\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A})=h^{\prime}.\mathbb{A}\cap h.\mathbb{A}=f^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})\cap f(\mathbb{A})=f(B).

By Proposition
5.50
,
B
is a finite intersection of affine half-apartments. By Proposition
5.46
, there is
w\in W_{a}
such that
g.p=w(p)
for all
p\in B
, so that for all
p\in B

f(p)=h.p=h^{\prime}g.p=h^{\prime}.w(p)=h^{\prime}.f_{0}(w(p))=(f^{\prime}\circ w)(p),

concluding the proof.
∎

Now we are able to describe the (not necessarily pointwise) stabilizer of
\mathbb{A}
.

Proposition 5.52
.

The (not necessarily pointwise) stabilizer of
\mathbb{A}
is
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A})=\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{F})=A_{\mathbb{F}}N_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Proof.

The inclusions
\supseteq
follow directly from the definitions. Now let
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
. By axiom (A2), there exists
w\in W_{a}
such that
g.f_{0}=f_{0}\circ w
. Let
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
and
k\in N_{\mathbb{F}}
with
ak.f_{0}=f_{0}\circ w=g.f_{0}
. Then
k^{-1}a^{-1}g\in A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}
by Theorem
5.19
. Then
g\in A_{\mathbb{F}}N_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}=A_{\mathbb{F}}N_{\mathbb{F}}
.
∎

5.9.
Axiom (A4)

In this section the root system
\Sigma
does not need to be reduced, except for being able to apply axiom (A2) in Lemma
5.53
. Axiom (A4) is a statement about sectors. Let
s_{0}=f_{0}(C_{0})\subseteq\mathcal{B}
be the fundamental sector corresponding to the fundamental Weyl chamber
C_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{A}
. All sectors are of the form
g.s_{0}
for some
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
. If a sector
s
is a subset of another sector
s^{\prime}
, then
s
is called a
subsector
of
s^{\prime}
.

From (A2) we get that subsectors of
s_{0}
are of the form
a.s_{0}
for
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
.

Lemma 5.53
.

For every subsector
s\subseteq s_{0}
there exists
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
s=a.s_{0}
.

Proof.

A general subsector of
s_{0}
is of the form
g.s_{0}
for some
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
. By Axiom (A2) we know that
s_{0}\to g.s_{0}
is realized by an element
w\in W_{a}
of the affine Weyl group,
g.b.o=w(b.o)
for all
b.o\in s_{0}
. We decompose
w=(t_{a},w_{s})\in\mathbb{A}\rtimes W_{s}
for some
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
. Since
o\in s_{0}
and
a.o=w(o)\in g.s_{0}\subseteq s_{0}
, we know that
\chi_{\alpha}(a)\geq 1
for all
\alpha>0
. We also know that
w_{s}(s_{0})
is one of the finitely many sectors based at
o
. If
w_{s}(s_{0})\neq s_{0}
, then there is some
\alpha>0
with
\chi_{\alpha}(b)\leq 1
for all
b.o\in w_{s}(s_{0})
. Since
w_{s}(s_{0})
is a cone with open interior, there are
b.o\in w_{s}(s_{0})
with arbitrary negative
\chi_{\alpha}(b)
, in particular there is some
b.o\in w_{s}(s_{0})
with
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(b))<(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a)^{-1})
, so that
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(ab))<0
. But this contradicts
a.w_{s}(s_{0})\subseteq s_{0}
, since
a.b.o\notin s_{0}
. We conclude that
w_{s}(s_{0})=s_{0}
and thus
g.s_{0}=a.s_{0}
.
∎

While studying the model apartment
\mathbb{A}
, Bennett
[
Ben94
, Prop 2.9]
proved the following lemma.

Lemma 5.54
.

For all
p\in\mathbb{A}
there is a
q\in\mathbb{A}
such that
q+C_{0}\subseteq(p+C_{0})\cap C_{0}
.

In our setting this lemma translates in terms of
A_{\mathbb{F}}.o=\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 5.55
.

For all
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
there is a
b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
b.s_{0}\subseteq a.s_{0}\cap s_{0}
.

We also get a slightly more general statement, illustrated in Figure
8
.

Lemma 5.56
.

For all subsectors
s^{\prime}\subseteq s_{0}
and for all
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
there is a subsector
s\subseteq a.s^{\prime}\cap s_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
s^{\prime}=\tilde{a}.s_{0}
for some
\tilde{a}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
, see Lemma
5.53
. We apply Lemma
5.55
with
a\tilde{a}
to get
b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
s:=b.s_{0}\subseteq a\tilde{a}.s_{0}\cap s_{0}=a.s^{\prime}\cap s_{0}
. ∎

Figure 8
.
Lemma
5.56
states that for every subsector
s^{\prime}\subseteq s_{0}
and
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
there is a sector
s
contained in both
s^{\prime}
and
a.s^{\prime}
.

We now use Proposition
5.12
to show that while elements of
U_{\mathbb{F}}
may not fix
s_{0}
itself, they at least fix a subsector.

Lemma 5.57
.

For every
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}
there is a subsector
s\subseteq s_{0}
with
u.p=p
for all
p\in s
.

Proof.

Let
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
a^{-1}ua\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
as in Proposition
5.12
. For all
b.o\in s_{0}
we have
a^{-1}ua.b.o=b.o
by Corollary
5.11
.
Therefore
u
fixes
a.s_{0}
pointwise. We don’t know whether
a.s_{0}
is a subsector of
s_{0}
, but we can apply Lemma
5.55
to find a subsector
s
of
s_{0}
, which is also a subsector of
a.s_{0}
and therefore is fixed pointwise by
u
.
∎

Now that we understand the action of
U_{\mathbb{F}}
better, let us turn to the group
B_{\mathbb{F}}=M_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}U_{\mathbb{F}}
introduced in
[
App25
, Section
]
, where
M_{\mathbb{F}}=\operatorname{Cen}_{K_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}}),A_{\mathbb{F}}
and
U_{\mathbb{F}}
are as before.

Lemma 5.58
.

For all
b\in B_{\mathbb{F}}=M_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}U_{\mathbb{F}}
there is a subsector
s\subseteq s_{0}
with
b.s\subseteq s_{0}
.

Proof.

We first recall the elements of
M_{\mathbb{F}}
fix all of
\mathbb{A}
pointwise. Let
m\in M_{\mathbb{F}}
,
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}
and
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
b=mua
. Using Lemma
5.57
, we find a subsector
s^{\prime}\subseteq s_{0}
which is fixed by
u
pointwise. Applying Lemma
5.56
to
s^{\prime}
and
a^{-1}
we get a subsector
s\subseteq s_{0}\cap a^{-1}.s^{\prime}
. We now have

b.s\subseteq b.a^{-1}.s^{\prime}=mu.s^{\prime}=m.s^{\prime}=s^{\prime}\subseteq s_{0},

as claimed.
∎

We are now ready to prove axiom (A4) using the Bruhat decomposition
G_{\mathbb{F}}=B_{\mathbb{F}}W_{s}B_{\mathbb{F}}

[
App25
, Theorem
]
.

Theorem 5.59
.

Axiom

(A4)

For any sectors
s_{1},s_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
there are subsectors
s_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq s_{1},s_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq s_{2}
such that there is an
f\in\mathscr{F}
with
s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2}^{\prime}\subseteq f(\mathbb{A})
.

holds for
(\mathcal{B},\mathscr{F})
.

Proof.

The action of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
on the sectors is transitive by definition and we may hence assume without loss of generality that one of the sectors in (A4) is
s_{0}
and the other is given by
g.s_{0}
for some
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
. We have to prove

(A4)’

For all
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
, there are subsectors
s\subseteq s_{0},s^{\prime}\subseteq g.s_{0}
such that there is an
f\in\mathscr{F}
with
s,s^{\prime}\subseteq f(\mathbb{A})
.

Figure 9
.
Axiom (A4) states that while the sectors
s_{0},g.s_{0}
may not lie in a common flat, they contain subsectors
s,s^{\prime}
contained in a common flat
f(\mathbb{A})
.

The situation is illustrated in Figure
9
. The Bruhat-decomposition
[
App25
, Theorem
]
, states that
G_{\mathbb{F}}
is a disjoint union

G_{\mathbb{F}}=\bigcup_{w=[k]\in W_{s}}B_{\mathbb{F}}kB_{\mathbb{F}}

of double cosets.
Let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
and
b,b^{\prime}\in B_{\mathbb{F}},k\in N_{\mathbb{F}}:=\operatorname{Nor}_{K_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
with
g=bkb^{\prime}
. We further decompose
b^{\prime}=ma^{\prime}ua
for
m\in M_{\mathbb{F}},u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
using
B_{\mathbb{F}}=M_{\mathbb{F}}U_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}
and Proposition
5.12
. Since
(ab)^{-1}\in B_{\mathbb{F}}
, apply Lemma
5.58
to obtain a subsector
\tilde{s}\subseteq s_{0}
with
(ab)^{-1}.\tilde{s}\subset s_{0}
. We apply Lemma
5.56
to
\tilde{s}
and
a^{-1}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
to obtain a subsector
s\subset a^{-1}.\tilde{s}\cap s_{0}
. We claim that
s
and
s^{\prime}:=g.s
are the required subsectors in (A4)’ and that
f=b.f_{0}
defines the common apartment.

We have by construction that
s\subseteq s_{0}
and clearly
s^{\prime}=g.s\subseteq g.s_{0}
. It remains to show that
s
and
s^{\prime}
are in the apartment
b.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
. We see

b^{-1}.s\subseteq b^{-1}.a^{-1}.\tilde{s}\subseteq s_{0}\subseteq f_{0}(\mathbb{A})

hence
s\subseteq b.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
and

\displaystyle s^{\prime}

\displaystyle=g.s=bkma^{\prime}ua.s\subseteq bkma^{\prime}u.\tilde{s}=bkma^{\prime}.\tilde{s}

\displaystyle\subseteq bkma.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})=bkm.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})=bk.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})=b.f_{0}(\mathbb{A}),

where we used Corollary
5.11
for
u.\tilde{s}=\tilde{s}
since
\tilde{s}\subseteq s_{0}
and
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
.
∎

5.10.
Axiom (EC)

In this section, the Jacobson-Morozov-construction as well as axiom (A2) is used, so we require
\Sigma
to be reduced. We recall that a
half-apartment in the model apartment
is a set of the form

H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))\geq\ell\right\}

for some
\alpha\in\Sigma
and some
\ell\in\Lambda
. A
half-apartment in the building
is
g.H_{\alpha}^{+}
for any
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
. We first use axiom (A2) to make sure that if a half-apartment of the building is included in
\mathbb{A}
, then it is an affine half-apartment in the model apartment.

Lemma 5.60
.

Let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}},\alpha\in\Sigma,\ell\in\Lambda
such that
g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}\subseteq\mathbb{A}
. Then there is
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Sigma,\ell^{\prime}\in\Lambda
such that

g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=H_{\alpha^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}^{+}.

Proof.

By axiom (A2), Theorem
5.51
, there is
w\in W_{a}
such that
g.H_{\alpha}^{+}=w(H_{\alpha}^{+})
. We decompose
w=(t_{a},w_{s})\in\mathbb{A}\rtimes W_{s}=W_{a}
and define
\alpha^{\prime}:=w_{s}(\alpha)
. Then
g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=w(H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+})=a.H_{\alpha^{\prime},\ell}^{+}=H_{\alpha^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}^{+}
for
\ell^{\prime}:=(-v)(\chi_{\alpha^{\prime}}(a))+\ell^{\prime}
.
∎

We start by proving axiom

(EC)

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{F}
, if
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
is a half-apartment, then there exists
f_{3}\in\mathscr{F}
such that
f_{i}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})
are half-apartments for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Moreover
f_{3}(\mathbb{A})
is the symmetric difference of
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})
and
f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
together with the boundary wall of
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
.

in the special case where

f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})=H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}:=\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))\geq\ell\right\}

for some
\alpha\in\Sigma
and
\ell\in\Lambda
, before deducing the full statement in Theorem
5.62
. The situation is illustrated in Figure
10
.

Proposition 5.61
.

Let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g^{-1}.\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}=H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}
for some
\alpha\in\Sigma,\ell\in\Lambda
. Then there exists an
h\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
h^{-1}.\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}=H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}
and
h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}
. Moreover there is some
n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
such that pointwise
g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}
and
h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}
.

Proof.

Since
g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=\mathbb{A}\cap g.\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{A}
, we can apply axiom (A2), Theorem
5.51
, to obtain
n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
such that
g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}
pointwise. Hence
n^{-1}g
fixes
H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}
pointwise and by Corollary
5.21
,
n^{-1}g\in U_{\alpha,\ell}A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}
. So let
u\in U_{\alpha,\ell}
such that
n^{-1}g.p=u.p
for all
p\in\mathbb{A}
. By Lemma
5.27
, the Jacobson-Morozov-homomorphism can be used to define

\displaystyle u^{\prime}=\varphi_{\mathbb{F}}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1/t&0\end{pmatrix}\in(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}

with
\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime})=-\varphi_{\alpha}(u)=-\ell
and
m(u)=u^{\prime}uu^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
which acts as the reflection along
M_{\alpha,\ell}=\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))=\ell\}
by Proposition
5.28
. For
h:=n(u^{\prime})^{-1}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
we then have

h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=n(u^{\prime})^{-1}m(u).H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=nuu^{\prime}.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=nu.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}

with
(\mathbb{A}\cap h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+})=g.M_{\alpha,\ell}=n.M_{\alpha,\ell}=h.M_{\alpha,\ell}\subseteq h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}
and therefore

h.(h^{-1}.\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A})=\mathbb{A}\cap h.\mathbb{A}=(\mathbb{A}\cap h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+})\cup(\mathbb{A}\cap h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-})=\mathbb{A}\cap h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}

which implies
h^{-1}.\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}=h^{-1}n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=u^{\prime}.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}
as required.
∎

Figure 10
.
Axiom (EC) states that if
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
is a half-apartment, then there exists
f_{3}\in\mathbb{F}
situated as illustrated. Proposition
5.61
deals with the case where the half-apartment is contained in
\mathbb{A}
, illustrated in the first two parts. Theorem
5.62
then tackles the general case of axiom (EC).

Theorem 5.62
.

Axiom

(EC)

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{F}
, if
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
is a half-apartment, then there exists
f_{3}\in\mathscr{F}
such that
f_{i}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})
are half-apartments for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Moreover
f_{3}(\mathbb{A})=f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})\cup f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})
and
\partial(f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A}))=f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})
.

holds.

Proof.

Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
f_{1}=g_{1}.f_{0}
and
f_{2}=g_{2}.f_{0}
. For
g:=g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}
, we have
f_{2}=g_{1}g.f_{0}
. Since
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})
is a half-apartment, so is

(g_{2})^{-1}(f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A}))=g_{2}^{-1}(g_{1}.\mathbb{A}\cap g_{2}.\mathbb{A})=g^{-1}.\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}\subseteq\mathbb{A}

and by Lemma
5.60
, there is some
\alpha\in\Sigma
and
\ell\in\Lambda
such that
g^{-1}.\mathbb{A}\cap\mathbb{A}=H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}
. Now we use Proposition
5.61
to obtain
h\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
and
n\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
with
g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}
,
h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}
and
h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}
. We define
g_{3}:=g_{1}h
and
f_{3}=g_{3}.f_{0}
.
We have as in Figure
10
.

\displaystyle g_{3}.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}

\displaystyle=g_{1}h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=g_{1}.n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}

\displaystyle g_{2}.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}

\displaystyle=g_{1}g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=g_{1}.n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}

\displaystyle g_{3}.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}

\displaystyle=g_{1}h.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}=g_{1}g.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}=g_{2}.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}

so
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})=f_{3}(H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-})
and
f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})=f_{3}(H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+})
are half-apartments and

f_{3}(\mathbb{A})=f_{3}(H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+})\cup f_{3}(H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-})=f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A})\cup f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A}).

The wall of the half-apartment
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})=g_{2}.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}
is given by

\displaystyle\partial(f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A}))

\displaystyle=g_{2}.M_{\alpha,\ell}=g_{1}g.M_{\alpha,\ell}=g_{1}.n.M_{\alpha,\ell}=g_{1}n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{+}\cap g_{1}n.H_{\alpha,\ell}^{-}

\displaystyle=f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{3}(\mathbb{A}).\qed

This concludes the proof of the last remaining axiom and the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

5.11.
Beyond reduced root systems

The main theorem of this article relies on the assumption that the root system
\Sigma
is reduced.

Theorem
5.1
.

If the root system
\Sigma
is reduced, then the pair
(\mathcal{B},\mathscr{F})
is an affine
\Lambda
-building.

We remark that
\mathcal{B}
is defined for any self-adjoint semisimple linear algebraic
\mathbb{K}
-group, independent of its root system. We expect Theorem
5.1
to still hold without the assumption on
\Sigma
.

Question 5.63
.

Is the pair
(\mathcal{B},\mathscr{F})
an affine
\Lambda
-building even when
\Sigma
is not reduced?

We outline here how our proof relies on the assumption, how the assumption cannot be removed using our strategy and a possible alternative proof strategy that might be of use to eliminate the assumption.

In our proof, the assumption first comes up in the Jacobson-Morozov Lemma, both in the setting of Lie algebras, see
[
App25
, Lemma
]
and in the semialgebraic setting, see
[
App25
, Proposition
]
. Explicit calculations in
\mathfrak{su}_{1,2}
show that
[
App25
, Lemma
]
does not hold and similarly,
[
App25
, Proposition
]
does not hold for
\operatorname{SU}(1,2)
. For given
\alpha\in\Sigma
and
X\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2\alpha}
, the task of the Jacobson-Morozov Lemma is to find
Y\in\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{-2\alpha}
and
H\in\mathfrak{a}\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{0}
such that
(X,Y,H)
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-triplet. While
Y
can be found as a multiple of
\theta(X)
, there is no guarantee that
H:=[X,Y]\in\mathfrak{a}
when
\mathfrak{g}_{2\alpha}\neq 0
.

In the proof of Theorem
5.1
, the Jacobson-Morozov Lemma is used to associate to each
u\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
an element
m(u)\in\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
representing a reflection as in Proposition
5.28
. We expect the following construction in the rank one subgroup
L_{\pm\alpha}
to give a definition of
m(u)
also when
\Sigma
is not reduced. We use the Bruhat-decomposition of
L_{\pm\alpha}
.

Corollary 5.64
.

Let
(B_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}:=(M_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(A_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
. Then there is a representative
m\in(N_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
of the unique non-trivial element in
W_{\pm\alpha}
, so that

(L_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}=(B_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\ \amalg\ (B_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cdot m\cdot(B_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}.

If
u\in(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
, let
u\in(B_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\ \amalg\ (B_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cdot m\cdot(B_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
and since
(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}\cap(B_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}=\{\operatorname{Id}\}
, we can find
b,b^{\prime}\in(B_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
u=bmb^{\prime}
. Writing
b=\bar{u}\bar{a}\bar{m}
and
b^{\prime}=\bar{m}^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}\bar{u}^{\prime}
with
\bar{m},\bar{m}^{\prime}\in(M_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}},\bar{a},\bar{a}^{\prime}\in(A_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\bar{u},\bar{u}^{\prime}\in(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}
, we define

m(u):=\bar{a}\bar{m}m\bar{m}^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}=\bar{u}^{-1}u(\bar{u}^{\prime})^{-1}\in\operatorname{Nor}_{(L_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}}((A_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}})\cap(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}(U_{-\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}.

We note that as a consequence of
[
App25
, Lemma
]
,
\operatorname{Nor}_{(L_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}}}((A_{\pm\alpha})_{\mathbb{F}})\subseteq\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})
. In the case where
\Sigma
is reduced, the uniqueness statement of Lemma
5.32
then shows that
m(u)
defined here agrees with
m(u)
defined via the Jacobson-Morozov Lemma. This suggests that the definition of
m(u)
as outlined here should be used when
\Sigma
is not reduced. However, our proof also relies on the explicit description of
m(u)
as
m(u)=u^{\prime}uu^{\prime\prime}
with
\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime})=\varphi_{-\alpha}(u^{\prime\prime})=-\varphi_{\alpha}(u)
, see the second part of Lemma
5.32
, which follows from the explicit description of the root group valuation in Lemma
5.26
relying heavily on the Jacobson-Morozov description. One way to allow a similar level of understanding of the root group valuations in the case of non-reduced root systems could be to do a case by case analysis of all rank one groups.

In the work of Bruhat Tits, the existence of such a
m(u)
is related to axiom (DR4) of a
donné radicielles

[
BT72
, (6.1.1)]
, which holds for even more general groups
[
BT72
, (6.1.3)c)]
and
[
BT65
]
. However, it is not clear how directly this is useful here, as we defined
\varphi_{\alpha}
in terms of matrix entries and we do not work with a Chevalley-basis.

6.
Residue building and building at infinity

In this section, we discuss the residue building and the spherical building at infinity of
\mathcal{B}
.
Let
(\mathcal{B},\mathscr{F})
be an affine
\Lambda
-building. Recall that the
Weyl chamber associated to a basis

\Delta\subseteq\Phi
is the set

C_{\Delta}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon\alpha(x)\geq 0\text{ for all }\alpha\in\Delta\right\}.

For
J\subseteq\Delta
, the subsets

C_{J}=\left\{x\in\mathbb{A}\colon\begin{array}[]{ll}\alpha(x)\geq 0&\text{for }\alpha\in J\\
\alpha(x)=0&\text{for }\alpha\in\Delta\setminus J\end{array}\right\}

are called
Weyl simplices
. For a chart
f\in\mathscr{F}
,
f(C_{\Delta})
and
f(C_{J})
are also called
sectors
and
sector facets based at

f(0)
. We say that two sector facets
f(C_{J})
,
f^{\prime}(C_{J}^{\prime})
both based at some point
p\in\mathcal{B}

share the same germ
if there exists
\varepsilon\in\Lambda_{>0}
such that
f(C_{J}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(0))=f^{\prime}(C_{J}^{\prime}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(0))
. The equivalence class of all sector facets sharing the same germ as some sector facet
s
is called the
germ of

s
and denoted by
\Delta_{p}s
. The set of all germs based at
p
is a spherical building
[
Sch09
, Theorem 5.17]
, called the
residue building
\Delta_{p}\mathcal{B}
. Every apartment
A\subseteq\mathcal{B}
containing
p
determines an apartment
\Delta_{p}A
in
\Delta_{p}\mathcal{B}
.

On the large scale, we call two sector facets
s,s^{\prime}

parallel
if there exists
s=s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{n}=s^{\prime}
sector facets such that
s_{i}=f_{i}(C_{J})
and
s_{i+1}=f_{i}\circ t_{i}(C_{J})
for some charts
f_{i}\in\mathscr{F}
and translations
t_{i}\in W_{a}
. The parallel class of a sector facet
s
is called
simplex at infinity
and is denoted by
\partial_{\infty}s
. The set of simplices at infinity is a spherical building
[
Ben94
, Section 3.4]
, called the
building at infinity

\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
. There is a one-to-one correspondence of apartments
A\subseteq\mathcal{B}
and apartments
\partial_{\infty}A
in
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
.

6.1.
Residue building

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the affine
\Lambda
-building defined in Section
4
,
C_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{A}
the fundamental Weyl chamber and
k\coloneqq O/J
the residue field as defined in Section
3.2
. Recall that a group acts
strongly transitively
on the spherical building
\Delta_{0}\mathcal{B}
, if it acts transitively on pairs
(\Delta_{o}s,\Delta_{o}A)
, where
s
is a sector based at
o
contained in an apartment
A
in
\mathcal{B}
. In this section, we will prove the following characterization of the residue building.

Theorem 6.1
.

The group
G_{k}
acts strongly transitively on the residue building
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
, which can therefore be identified with the spherical building associated to the BN-pair
B=B_{k}=U_{k}A_{k}M_{k}=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}C_{0})
and
N=\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{k}}(A_{k})=A_{k}N_{k}=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}\mathbb{A})
.

By
[
EP05
, Theorem 4.3.7]
, the residue field
k
of a real closed field
\mathbb{F}
is real closed. For any semialgebraic set
V_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
, the entrywise reduction
\pi\colon V_{\mathbb{F}}(O)\coloneq V_{\mathbb{F}}\cap O^{n\times n}\to V_{k}
is well-defined and surjective
[
BIPP25
, Proposition 2.18]
, in particular we obtain a surjective group homomorphism

\displaystyle\pi\colon G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)\twoheadrightarrow G_{k}.

By Theorem
4.19
,
G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(o)
acts on the set of sector facets based at
o
. Since it acts by isometries, it preserves the equivalence relation of sharing the same germ, hence
G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
acts on the residue building
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
. The following Lemma implies that this action descends to an action of
G_{k}
on
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 6.2
.

The subgroup
\operatorname{ker}(G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)\twoheadrightarrow G_{k})
acts trivially on
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Let
g\in\ker(G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)\twoheadrightarrow G_{k})
, which means that the matrix
g-\operatorname{Id}
has entries in the maximal ideal
J
. In the language of Section
4.5
, where we defined
(-v)(M)\coloneqq\max_{ij}\{(-v)(M_{ij})\}
for
M\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}
this means
(-v)(g-\operatorname{Id})<0
.
Now let
C^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}
be the constant from Lemma
4.17
such that whenever
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
satisfies
(-v)(\chi_{\delta}(a))\leq\lambda
for all
\delta\in\Delta
and some
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\geq 0}
, then
(-v)(a)\leq C^{\prime}\ \lambda
. Let
\lambda\in\Lambda_{>0}
be such that

(-v)(g-\operatorname{Id})\leq-2C^{\prime}\lambda.

We will show that
g
fixes all points
p\in\mathcal{B}
with
d(p,o)\leq\lambda
, which then shows the result.

Let
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
,
k\in K_{\mathbb{F}}
so that
p=ka.o
by the Cartan decomposition
[
App25
, Theorem
]
. We have

\displaystyle\lambda\geq d(p.o,o)

\displaystyle=d(a.o,o)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma}\left\lvert(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))\right\rvert,

so in particular
(-v)(\chi_{\delta}(a))\leq\lambda
for all
\delta\in\Delta
. Thus
(-v)(a)\leq C^{\prime}\lambda
. By Lemma
4.16
,

(-v)(ka)\leq(-v)(k)+(-v)(a)=(-v)(a)\leq C^{\prime}\lambda

and since
ka
has determinant
1
, the inverse is given by the adjugate matrix whose entries have the same valuations, so

(-v)\left((ka)^{-1}\right)=(-v)(ka)\leq C^{\prime}\lambda.

Now
g
fixes
p
if and only if
gka.o=ka.o
, or equivalently
(ka)^{-1}gka\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
by Theorem
4.19
, and

\displaystyle(-v)\left((ka)^{-1}gka\right)

\displaystyle=(-v)\left((ka)^{-1}(g-\operatorname{Id})ka+(ka)^{-1}\operatorname{Id}ka\right)

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{(-v)\left((ka)^{-1}(g-\operatorname{Id})ka\right),(-v)(\operatorname{Id})\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{(-v)\left((ka)^{-1}\right)+(-v)(g-\operatorname{Id})+(-v)(ka),0\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\left\{C^{\prime}\lambda-2C^{\prime}\lambda+C^{\prime}\lambda,0\right\}=0

where we used Lemma
4.16
.
∎

The following Lemma will help us with the computation of the stabilizers.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\Sigma_{>0}
be the positive roots with respect to some order. If
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}
stabilizes
o
and a point
a.o\in\mathbb{A}
with
\chi_{\alpha}(a)\in J
, for all
\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}
, then
u\in\operatorname{ker}(\pi\colon G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)\twoheadrightarrow G_{k})
.

Proof.

Let
\Omega=\{o,a.o\}
, then
U_{\Omega}^{+}=\langle u^{\prime}\in U_{\alpha,\Omega}\colon\alpha>0\rangle
by Proposition
5.40
. For all
\alpha\in\Sigma_{>0}
,
u^{\prime}\in U_{\alpha,\Omega}
, we have

\varphi_{\alpha}(u^{\prime})\leq(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))<0,

in particular
(u^{\prime}-\operatorname{Id})_{ij}\in J
for all
i,j
by Lemma
5.6
. Since
u^{\prime}u^{\prime\prime}-\operatorname{Id}=(u^{\prime}-\operatorname{Id})(u^{\prime\prime}-\operatorname{Id})+(u^{\prime}-\operatorname{Id})+(u^{\prime\prime}-\operatorname{Id})
, we have
(u-\operatorname{Id})_{ij}\in J
for all
u\in U^{+}
. In particular
\pi(u)=\operatorname{Id}\in G_{k}
, which is what we claimed.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Let
s,s^{\prime}
be sectors based at
o
contained in apartments
A,A^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. To check that
G_{k}
acts strongly transitively on
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
, it suffices to find an element
h\in G_{k}
with
h.\Delta_{o}s=\Delta_{o}s^{\prime}
and
h.\Delta_{o}A=\Delta_{o}A^{\prime}
.
By
[
BSS14
]
, the following axiom holds for affine
\Lambda
-buildings.

(GG)

Any two germs of sectors based at the same vertex are contained in a common apartment.

We apply (GG) to find an apartment
A^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
that contains the germs
\Delta_{o}s
and
\Delta_{o}s^{\prime}
.
Since
G_{F}
acts transitively on apartments in
\mathcal{B}
,
G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
acts transitively on apartments containing
o
. Therefore
G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
and
G_{k}
also act transitively on germs of apartments in
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
.
Let
g\in G_{k}
be such that
g.\Delta_{o}A=\Delta_{o}A^{\prime\prime}
and up to precomposing with an element in
N_{k}
, we may assume that
g
stabilizes
\Delta_{o}s
. Similarly, let
g^{\prime}\in G_{k}
such that
g^{\prime}.\Delta_{o}A^{\prime\prime}=\Delta_{o}A^{\prime}
with
g^{\prime}.\Delta_{o}s^{\prime}=\Delta_{o}s^{\prime}
. Let
g^{\prime\prime}\in G_{k}
be an element that stabilizes the apartment
\Delta_{o}A^{\prime\prime}
, but such that
g^{\prime\prime}.\Delta_{o}s=\Delta_{o}s^{\prime}
. Note that
h\coloneq g^{\prime}g^{\prime\prime}g\in G_{k}
satisfies
h.\Delta_{o}s=\Delta_{o}s^{\prime}
and
h.\Delta_{o}A=\Delta_{o}A^{\prime\prime}
, proving that
G_{k}
acts strongly transitively on
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
.

By
[
Ron89
, (5.2), (5.3) and Remark 2]
the strongly transitive action of
G_{k}
determines the BN-pair
B=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}C_{0})
and
N=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}\mathbb{A})
, which in turn determines a building, which is isomorphic to
\Delta_{o}\mathcal{B}
.

We now compute
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{0}s_{0})
and
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}\mathbb{A})
. Let
\overline{g}\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}s_{0})
. By
[
BIPP25
, Proposition 2.18]
, there is a
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)}(\Delta_{0}s_{0})
with
\pi(g)=\overline{g}
. There exists an
\varepsilon\in\Lambda_{>0}
such that
g.f_{0}(C_{0}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(0))=f_{0}(C_{0}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(0))
. Actually by Axiom (A2) and the fact that
W_{s}
acts simply transitively on Weyl-chambers,
g
stabilizes
\Omega\coloneqq f_{0}(C_{0}\cap B_{\varepsilon}(0))
pointwise. By Theorem
5.49
,
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)}(\Omega)=U_{\Omega}^{-}U_{\Omega}^{+}N_{\Omega}
. We note that by Proposition
4.12
, there is a point
p=a.o\in\Omega
with
(-v)(\chi_{\alpha}(a))>0
, so we can apply Lemma
6.3
with respect to the negative order on
\Sigma
to see that
U_{\Omega}^{-}\subseteq\ker(\pi\colon G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)\twoheadrightarrow G_{k})
. Writing
g=u^{-}u^{+}n
for
u^{-}\in U_{\Omega}^{-}
,
u^{+}\in U_{\Omega}^{+}
and
n\in N_{\Omega}
, we notice that
n\in A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}
by Theorem
5.19
. We have
\overline{g}=\pi(u^{-})\pi(u^{+})\pi(n)=\pi(u^{+})\pi(n)\in U_{k}^{+}A_{k}M_{k}
. The other direction
B_{k}=U_{k}^{+}A_{k}M_{k}\subseteq\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}s_{0})
follows from Theorem
5.17
, which states that
B_{\mathbb{F}}(O)=U_{\mathbb{F}}(O)A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)M_{\mathbb{F}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(s_{0})
.

If now
\overline{g}\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}\mathbb{A})
, there exists
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)}(\Delta_{o}\mathbb{A})
with
\pi(g)=\overline{g}
. Let
\varepsilon\in\Lambda_{>0}
such that
g.f_{0}(B_{\varepsilon}(0))=f_{0}(B_{\varepsilon}(0))
. By axiom (A2) and Proposition
5.52
there is some
n\in A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)N_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
n^{-1}g
stabilises
\Omega\coloneqq f_{0}(B_{\varepsilon}(0))
pointwise. Then by Theorem
5.49
,
n^{-1}g\in U_{\Omega}^{-}U_{\Omega}^{+}N_{\Omega}
where we can apply Lemma
6.3
to both the positive and negative order on
\Sigma
to see that
\pi(U_{\Omega}^{+})=\pi(U_{\Omega}^{-})=\{\operatorname{Id}\}\subseteq G_{k}
. Thus
\overline{g}\in\pi(N_{\Omega})\pi(n)\subseteq A_{k}N_{k}=\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{k}}(A_{k})
, see Proposition
5.52
. The other direction
\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{k}}(A_{k})=A_{k}N_{k}\subseteq\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{k}}(\Delta_{o}\mathbb{A})
follows directly since
A_{\mathbb{F}}(O)N_{\mathbb{F}}
acts on
\mathbb{A}
preserving
o
.
∎

6.2.
Building at infinity

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the affine
\Lambda
-building defined in Section
4
.
In this section, we will prove the following characterization of the building at infinity.

Theorem 6.4
.

The group
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts strongly transitively on the building at infinity
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
, which can therefore be identified with the spherical building associated to the BN-pair
B=B_{\mathbb{F}}=U_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}M_{\mathbb{F}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}s_{0})
and
N=\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})=A_{\mathbb{F}}N_{\mathbb{F}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{A})
.

Proof.

Since
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts both on
\mathcal{B}
, but also on
\mathscr{F}
, the action preserves parallelism. Hence
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts on the building at infinity
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
. Let
s,s^{\prime}
be sectors contained in apartments
A,A^{\prime}
such that
(\partial_{\infty}s,\partial_{\infty}A),(\partial_{\infty}s^{\prime},\partial_{\infty}A^{\prime})
are pairs at infinity such that
\partial_{\infty}s\subseteq\partial_{\infty}A
and
\partial_{\infty}s^{\prime}\subseteq\partial_{\infty}A^{\prime}
.
By the definition of apartments,
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts transitively on apartments in
\mathcal{B}
, hence also on apartments in
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
, so let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g.A=A^{\prime}
. Now
g^{-1}.s^{\prime}
is some Weyl chamber in
A
. By the definition of Weyl chambers,
W_{a}
acts transitively on Weyl chambers, so there exists an element
w\in W_{a}
such that
w.s=g^{-1}.s^{\prime}
. By axiom (A1) there exists
g^{\prime}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g^{\prime}.f=g.f\circ w
, so
g^{\prime}.A=A^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}.s=s^{\prime}
. This shows that
G_{\mathbb{F}}
acts strongly transitively on
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
.

By
[
Ron89
, (5.2), (5.3) and Remark 2]
the strongly transitive action of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
determines the BN-pair
B=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}s_{0})
and
N=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{A})
, which in turn determines a building, which is isomorphic to
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
.

We now compute
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}s_{0})
and
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{A})
. Let
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}s_{0})
. This means that there are sectors
s_{0},s_{1},\ldots,s_{k}\eqqcolon g.s_{0}
and charts
f_{i}\in\mathscr{F}
and translations
t_{i}\in T=\mathbb{A}
for
i\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,k-1\}
with
s_{i}=f_{i}(C_{0})
and
s_{i+1}=f_{i}(t_{i}(C_{0}))
. Let
a_{i}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
a_{i}.f_{0}=f_{0}\circ t_{i}
and let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
f_{i}=g_{i}.f_{0}
. Notice
g_{0}=\operatorname{Id}\in B_{\mathbb{F}}
and
g_{k}=g
. We prove by induction that if
g_{i}\in B_{\mathbb{F}}
, then also
g_{i+1}\in B_{\mathbb{F}}
: we have

g_{i+1}.f_{0}(C_{0})=s_{i+1}=g_{i}.f_{0}(t_{i}(C_{0}))=g_{i}a_{i}.f_{0}(C_{0}),

so
g_{i+1}^{-1}g_{i}a_{i}\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(f_{0}(C_{0}))\subseteq B_{\mathbb{F}}
, by Theorem
5.17
(note that the pointwise and setwise stabilizers of
f_{0}(C_{0})
coincide). Since
a_{i}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq B_{\mathbb{F}}
and
g_{i}\in B_{\mathbb{F}}
by the induction assumption, we also have
g_{i+1}\in B_{\mathbb{F}}
.
The other direction
B_{\mathbb{F}}=U_{\mathbb{F}}A_{\mathbb{F}}M_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}s_{0})
follows from Theorem
5.17
and Proposition
5.12
.

Now let
g\in\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{A})
. Then
\partial_{\infty}(g.\mathbb{A})=g.\partial_{\infty}(\mathbb{A})=\partial_{\infty}(\mathbb{A})
, which is equivalent to
g.\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}
, since there is a one-to-one correspondence of apartments in
\mathcal{B}
and in
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{B}
. Then by Proposition
5.52
,
\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{A})=\operatorname{Stab}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(\mathbb{A})=\operatorname{Nor}_{G_{\mathbb{F}}}(A_{\mathbb{F}})=A_{\mathbb{F}}N_{\mathbb{F}}
.
∎

7.
Appendix: The building for
\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{F})

To obtain Theorem
5.1
, that
\mathcal{B}
is an affine
\Lambda
-building, a relatively large amount of effort goes into proving axiom (A2). The development of the theory following
[
BT72
]
in Subsections
5.5
,
5.6
and
5.7
is not needed when the group
G
is well understood. In this appendix, we give an alternative proof of axiom (A2) in the case where
G_{\mathbb{F}}=\operatorname{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{F})
. The proof still relies on the general theory developed for semialgebraic groups in
[
App25
, Sections
and
]
, but is significantly shorter.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a non-Archimedean real closed field with order compatible valuation
(-v)\colon\mathbb{F}\to\Lambda\cup\{-\infty\}
and valuation ring
O=\{a\in\mathbb{F}\colon(-v)(a)\leq 0\}
. We consider the semisimple linear algebraic group
G=\operatorname{SL}_{n}
with maximal
\mathbb{K}
-split torus

S=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}\star&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\star\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{SL}_{n}\right\}.

Then the groups showing up in the various decompositions of
[
App25
, Section
]
are given by

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{SO}_{n}(\mathbb{F})

\displaystyle A_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\{a=(a_{ij})\in S_{\mathbb{F}}\colon a_{ii}>0\right\}

\displaystyle U_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\{g=(g_{ij})\in\operatorname{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{F})\colon g_{ii}=1,\,g_{ij}=0\text{ for }i>j\right\}

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\text{ permutation matrices with entries in }\pm 1\right\}

\displaystyle M_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\{a=(a_{ij})\in S_{\mathbb{F}}\colon a_{ii}\in\{\pm 1\}\right\}

\displaystyle B_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle=\left\{g=(g_{ij})\in\operatorname{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{F})\colon g_{ij}=0\text{ for }i>j\right\}.

Then
\mathfrak{a}=\operatorname{Lie}(A_{\mathbb{R}})=\{H\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\colon\operatorname{tr}(H)=0\text{ and $H$ is diagonal }\}
and the root system
\Sigma
associated with
\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{R})
is given by
\Sigma=\{\alpha_{ij}\in\mathfrak{a}^{\star}\colon i\neq j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\}
for the roots

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}\colon\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle\mapsto H_{ii}-H_{jj}.

The spherical Weyl group
W_{s}
is then isomorphic to the symmetric group
S_{n}
on
n
letters. If we choose the ordered basis
\Delta=\{\alpha_{12},\ldots,\alpha_{(n-1)n}\}
, we obtain the positive Weyl chamber

A_{\mathbb{F}}^{+}=\{\operatorname{Diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in A_{\mathbb{F}}\colon a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\ldots\geq a_{n}\}.

As in Section
4
we define the non-standard symmetric space

P_{1}(n,\mathbb{F}):=\left\{A\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}\colon A{{}^{\mathsf{T}}\!}=A,\ \det(A)=1,\ A\text{ is positive definite }\right\}

and note that
\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{F})
acts transitively on
P_{1}(n,\mathbb{F})
. The multiplicative norm

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{F}}\colon A_{\mathbb{F}}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{F}_{\geq 1}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Diag}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})

\displaystyle\mapsto\prod_{i\neq j}\max\left\{\frac{a_{i}}{a_{j}},\frac{a_{j}}{a_{i}}\right\}

then gives a
G_{\mathbb{F}}
-invariant
\Lambda
-pseudo distance
d=(-v)\circ N_{\mathbb{F}}\circ\delta_{\mathbb{F}}
which allows us to define the
\Lambda
-metric space
\mathcal{B}:=P_{1}(n,\mathbb{F})/\!\!\sim
. We endow
\mathcal{B}
with the apartment structure
\mathscr{F}=\{g.f_{0}\colon g\in\operatorname{SL}(n,\mathbb{F})\}
, where
f_{0}\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is the inclusion of the apartment
\mathbb{A}:=A_{\mathbb{F}}.o
for the basepoint
o=[\operatorname{Id}]\in\mathcal{B}
. The following is a special case of Theorem
5.1
.

Theorem 7.1
.

The
\Lambda
-metric space
\mathcal{B}
is an affine
\Lambda
-building of type
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}(\Sigma^{\vee},\Lambda,\Lambda^{n})
, where
\Sigma^{\vee}
is a root system of type
\operatorname{A}_{n-1}
.

The axioms (A1), (A3) and (TI) follow as described in Section
5.1
. We will give a hands on proof of axiom (A2) that only relies on Theorem
4.19
about the stabilizer of
o\in\mathcal{B}
. Axiom (A4) can then be proved as in Section
5.9
, relying on (A2) and the fact that every
u\in U_{\mathbb{F}}
stabilizes a sector, which can be proven as in Section
5.3
, or looking at matrix entries directly. Finally, axiom (EC) relies on (A2) and the fact that Jacobson-Morozov morphisms can be found, which can be seen for
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{F})
explicitly.

We first look at the axiom

(A2)

For all
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{F}
, if
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\neq\emptyset
, then the set
\Omega:=f_{2}^{-1}\circ f_{1}(\mathbb{A})
is a
W_{a}
-convex set and there exists
w\in W_{a}
such that
f_{2}|_{\Omega}=f_{1}\circ w|_{\Omega}
.

in the special case where
f_{2}=f_{0}
and
f_{1}=g.f_{0}
for some
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
g.o=o
. The set
\Omega
is defined by
f_{0}(\Omega)=f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
. Let
B\subset A_{\mathbb{F}}
be the set of all elements
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
g.a.o\in f_{0}(\Omega)
. Note that elements in
B
are diagonal matrices whose diagonal entries are positive elements in the real closed field
\mathbb{F}
, we can thus take their
n
-th roots. We would like to prove that
\Omega
is a finite intersection of closed affine half-apartments. The reason we define
W_{a}
-convexity this way is that general convex combinations of points in the apartment
\mathbb{A}
are not possible, since we do not have a vector space-structure on
\mathbb{A}
. Some convex combinations however are still possible and
\Omega
contains them.

Lemma 7.2
.

For all
a,a^{\prime}\in B
and all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sqrt[n+m]{a^{n}a^{\prime m}}\in B
.

Proof.

Let
b,b^{\prime}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g.a.o=b.o
and
g.a^{\prime}.o=b^{\prime}.o
. By Theorem
4.19

b^{-1}ga,(b^{\prime})^{-1}ga^{\prime}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
. We can now exploit the explicit structure of
A_{\mathbb{F}}
to write in coordinates

g_{ij}\frac{a_{j}}{b_{i}}\in O\quad\text{and}\quad g_{ij}\frac{a^{\prime}_{j}}{b^{\prime}_{i}}\in O.

Thus also

g_{ij}^{n+m}\frac{a_{j}^{n}{a^{\prime}}_{j}^{m}}{b_{i}^{n}{b^{\prime}}_{i}^{m}}\in O\quad\text{and so}\quad g_{ij}\frac{\sqrt[n+m]{a_{j}^{n}{a^{\prime}}_{j}^{m}}}{\sqrt[n+m]{b_{i}^{n}{b^{\prime}}_{i}^{m}}}\in O.

We note that if
a=\operatorname{Diag}\left(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\right)\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
, then also

\sqrt[n+m]{a}:=\operatorname{Diag}(\sqrt[n+m]{a_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt[n+m]{a_{n}})\in A_{\mathbb{F}},

since this is a first order statement that is true over the reals. Then

g.\sqrt[n+m]{a^{n}a^{\prime m}}.o=\sqrt[n+m]{b^{n}b^{\prime m}}.o\in\mathbb{A}

completes the proof.
∎

We now introduce a notion of regularity. Elements
a.o\in f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
with
a\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
are represented by diagonal matrices with entries
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{F}
. Consider the amount of distinct entries
(-v)(a_{1}),\ldots,(-v)(a_{n})
as elements in
\Lambda=(-v)(\mathbb{F}_{>0})
. Let
\boldsymbol{a}\in B
be a maximal element with respect to the amount of distinct entries. The intuition is that elements with
a_{i}=a_{j}
for
i\neq j
lie on a wall. The more walls they lie on, the less regular they are. Other elements in
B
may have as many distinct entries as
\boldsymbol{a}
but the next Lemma states that those are the same ones as the ones for
\boldsymbol{a}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
b\in B
. If
(-v)(b_{i})\neq(-v)(b_{j})
for some
i,j
, then
(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{i})\neq(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{j})
. Equivalently if
(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{i})=(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{j})
, then
(-v)(b_{i})=(-v)(b_{j})
.

Proof.

For any element
b\in B
, we define
I_{b}=\{(i,j)\colon(-v)(b_{i})\neq(-v)(b_{j})\}
. The maximality of
\boldsymbol{a}
means that
|I_{b}|\leq|I_{\boldsymbol{a}}|
for all
b\in B
. We want to prove that
I_{b}\subseteq I_{\boldsymbol{a}}
for all
b\in B
. We note that there are certainly less than
n^{2}
elements in
I_{\boldsymbol{a}}
, and define for
k\in\{0,1,\ldots,n^{2}\}
the element

c(k)=\sqrt[n^{2}]{\boldsymbol{a}^{k}b^{n^{2}-k}}\in B

which is in
B
by Lemma
7.2
. Let
(i,j)\in I_{\boldsymbol{a}}\cup I_{b}
. Thus
(-v)(\boldsymbol{a_{i}}/\boldsymbol{a_{j}})\neq 0
or
(-v)(b_{i}/b_{j})\neq 0
. We define the map

k\mapsto(-v)(c(k)_{i}/c(k)_{j})\in\Lambda

which is either constant, but not
=0
, or strictly monotonous ascending or descending. Either way, there is at most one value of
k
for which the map is
0
. Let
k_{ij}
be this value, if it exists.

Now pick a
k
which does not appear as
k_{ij}
for any
(i,j)\in I_{\boldsymbol{a}}\cup I_{b}
. This means that
c(k)_{i}\neq c(k)_{j}
for all
(i,j)\in I_{\boldsymbol{a}}\cup I_{b}
. We conclude that
I_{\boldsymbol{a}}\cup I_{b}\subseteq I_{c(k)}
. But since
\boldsymbol{a}
is maximal, we have
I_{\boldsymbol{a}}=I_{c(k)}
and
I_{b}\subseteq I_{\boldsymbol{a}}
.
∎

Recall that
O\subsetneqq\mathbb{F}
is a strict subring of a non-Archimedean real closed field
\mathbb{F}
. Denote the units of
O
by
O^{\times}
. The following is a standard fact about valuation rings.

Lemma 7.4
.

In any valuation ring
O
, the set of nonunits
O\setminus O^{\times}
is an ideal.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in O\setminus O^{\times},r\in O
. Since
x
is not a unit,
x^{-1}=r\cdot(rx)^{-1}\not\in O
. Therefore
(rx)^{-1}\not\in O
, so
rx\in O\setminus O^{\times}
. If
x
and
y
are nonzero, then
x/y\in O
or
y/x\in O
, since
O
is a valuation ring. Writing
x+y=y\cdot(1+x/y)=x\cdot(1+y/x)
, we see that also
x+y\in O\setminus O^{\times}
.
∎

The determinant of a matrix is a polynomial of its entries. By the previous Lemma, if all entries of a matrix are in
O\setminus O^{\times}
, then so is its determinant. We conclude that if the determinant of a matrix with entries in
O
is
1
, then at least one entry has to be a unit. Actually, even more holds.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
g
be a matrix with entries in
O
and
\operatorname{det}(g)=1
. Then in every row and every column there exists at least one entry that is a unit. In fact, there is a permutation
\sigma
, such that
g_{i\sigma(i)}\in O^{\times}
.

Proof.

Consider the
i
’th row
(g_{i1},g_{i2},\ldots,g_{in})
of
g
. Using Laplace’s formula for the determinant

\det(g)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(-1)^{i+j}g_{ij}\cdot\det M_{ij},

where
M_{ij}
are the minor matrices after deleting row
i
and column
j
, we see that since
\det(g)=1\in O^{\times}
