, at least one of the
g_{ij}
for
j=1,\ldots,n
has to be a unit. Equivalently there has to be a unit in every column. We can extract a permutation by alternating the row and column argument.
∎

We will use Lemma
7.3
and Lemma
7.5
to prove a certain rigidity of the action of
G_{\mathbb{F}}
on
f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
, namely when an isometry
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
fixes the identity, then its action on
f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
can be described by an element in the spherical Weyl group
W_{s}
. The apartment
\mathbb{A}
itself could have many other symmetries, but the isometry can only be extended to all of
\mathcal{B}
, when it is one of the finitely many elements in
W_{s}
. Consider the fundamental sector

s_{0}=\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon a_{1}\geq\ldots\geq a_{n}\}

based at
o
. The sectors in
f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
based at
o
are fundamental domains of the action of
W_{s}
, see for instance
[
Hum72
, Chapter 10.3]
. So two points that lie inside a common sector based at
o
can only be related by an element in
W_{s}
if they are the same. This implies a first restricted version of axiom (A2).

Proposition 7.6
.

Let as before
\boldsymbol{a}\in B
be maximal with respect to the amount of distinct entries and
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g.o=o
. If both
\boldsymbol{a}.o
and
g.\boldsymbol{a}.o
lie in the same sector based at
o
, then
\boldsymbol{a}.o=g.\boldsymbol{a}.o
and moreover the action of
g
is the identity on the set
\Omega:=f_{0}^{-1}\left(f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\right)
, i.e.
f_{0}|_{\Omega}=g.f_{0}|_{\Omega}
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{a}=\operatorname{Diag}\left(\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{n}\right)
and
\boldsymbol{b}=\operatorname{Diag}\left(\boldsymbol{b}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{b}_{n}\right)\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
g.\boldsymbol{a}.o=\boldsymbol{b}.o
. Since
\boldsymbol{a}.o
and
\boldsymbol{b}.o
lie in the same sector based at
o
, their entries satisfy the same ordering. Formally we can find a permutation
\rho
, such that

(7.1)

\displaystyle(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\rho(1)})

\displaystyle\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\rho(2)})\leq\ldots\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\rho(n)})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{\rho(1)})

\displaystyle\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{\rho(2)})\leq\ldots\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{\rho(n)}).

From
g.o=o
follows that
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
by Proposition
4.19
. By the previous Lemma
7.5
we get a permutation
\sigma
, such that
g_{i\sigma(i)}\in O^{\times}
. In coefficients,
\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}g\boldsymbol{a}\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
implies
\boldsymbol{b}_{i}^{-1}g_{i\sigma(i)}\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(i)}\in O
and therefore also
\boldsymbol{b}_{i}^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(i)}\in O
. This means that
\operatorname{Diag}(\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(n)}).o=\operatorname{Diag}\left(\boldsymbol{b}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{b}_{n}\right).o
. So although the elements
\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(i)}
and
\boldsymbol{b}_{i}
may not be exactly the same, they satisfy
(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(i)})=(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i})
. So we can order the entries as

(7.2)

\displaystyle(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{\rho(1)})

\displaystyle\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{\rho(2)})

\displaystyle\leq\ldots\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{\rho(n)})\quad\text{and thus}

=

=

=

\displaystyle(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(\rho(1))})

\displaystyle\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(\rho(2))})

\displaystyle\leq\ldots\leq(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(\rho(n))}).

But we already have a decreasing ordering of the diagonal entries of
\boldsymbol{a}
in equation (
7.1
), so they have to be the same, i.e. for all
i
,
(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\rho(i)})=(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(\rho(i))})
and thus
(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{i})=(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{\sigma(i)})=(-v)(\boldsymbol{b}_{i})\in\Lambda
. We now know that
\boldsymbol{a}.o=\boldsymbol{b}.o
, but we still need to show that
g
is the identity on all of
\Omega
.

If all the inequalities in (
7.2
) were strict, then
\sigma
would necessarily be the identity. For indices
i,j
where there is an equality,
\sigma
could change these entries. However by Lemma
7.3
we know that whenever
(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{i})=(-v)(\boldsymbol{a}_{j})
, then also
(-v)(a_{i})=(-v)(a_{j})
for any
a=\operatorname{Diag}\left(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\right)\in B
. So for all
i
,
(-v)(a_{\sigma(i)})=(-v)(a_{i})
. In fact we see

g_{i\sigma(i)}\frac{a_{\sigma(i)}}{a_{i}}\in g_{i\sigma(i)}O^{\times}\subseteq O

and thus
g.a.o=a.o
for all
a\in B
, so
f_{0}|_{\Omega}=g.f_{0}|_{\Omega}
.
∎

We will now allow slightly more general points
\boldsymbol{a}.o
and
\boldsymbol{b}.o
.

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
g.o=o\in\mathcal{B}
, then there exists an element
w\in W_{s}
such that
f_{0}|_{\Omega}=g.f_{0}\circ w|_{\Omega}
, where
\Omega:=f_{0}^{-1}\left(f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\right)
.

Proof.

Use Lemma
7.3
to get
\boldsymbol{a}\in B
maximal with respect to the amount of distinct entries. Let
\boldsymbol{b}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
such that
\boldsymbol{b}.o=g.\boldsymbol{a}.o\in f_{0}(\Omega)
. Since the sectors in
f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
are fundamental domains for
W_{s}
, there is a
w\in W_{s}
such that
w.\boldsymbol{b}.o
and
\boldsymbol{a}.o
are in the same sector. Equivalently
\boldsymbol{b}.o
and
w^{-1}.\boldsymbol{a}.o
are in the same sector. Note that
gw.o=o
,
\boldsymbol{b}.o=(gw).(w^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}).o
,
f_{0}(\Omega)=f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})=f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap(gw).f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
(since
w.\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{A}
) and
w^{-1}\boldsymbol{a}
is maximal with respect to the amount of distinct entries, so we can apply Proposition
7.6
to get
f_{0}|_{\Omega}=(gw).f_{0}|_{\Omega}=g.f_{0}\circ w|_{\Omega}
.
∎

To show that axiom (A2) holds, there is one more obstacle, namely
g
may not preserve
o
in general. In fact,
\Omega
may not even contain
o
, so we have to first translate
\Omega
to be able to use the previous propositions.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
g\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
and define
\Omega:=f_{0}^{-1}\left(f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\right)
. Then there exists an element
\overline{w}\in W_{a}
such that
f_{0}|_{\Omega}=g.f_{0}\circ\overline{w}|_{\Omega}
.

Proof.

If
\Omega=\emptyset
, then any element of
W_{a}
will suffice. Otherwise choose and fix
\boldsymbol{a}\in B
(not necessarily maximal) and
\boldsymbol{b}\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
\boldsymbol{b}.o=g.\boldsymbol{a}.o\in f_{0}(\Omega)
. Now consider any
a,b\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
with
b.o=g.a.o\in f_{0}(\Omega)
. We translate the problem by
\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}
and get

\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.b.o=(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.g.\boldsymbol{a}).\boldsymbol{a}^{-1}.a.o

where
(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.g.\boldsymbol{a}).o=o
. We are almost in the situation of Proposition
7.7
, but we have a different
\Omega
. In fact since
\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})=f_{0}(\mathbb{A})=\boldsymbol{a}.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})
,

\displaystyle f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.g.\boldsymbol{a}).f_{0}(\mathbb{A})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.f_{0}(\Omega)=f_{0}(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.\Omega),

we can apply Proposition
7.7
to get a
w\in W_{s}
such that

f_{0}|_{\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.\Omega}=(\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.g.\boldsymbol{a}).f_{0}\circ w|_{\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.\Omega}\ \ ,

which can be rewritten as

\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.f_{0}|_{\Omega}=\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}.g.f_{0}\circ(\boldsymbol{a}w\boldsymbol{b}^{-1})|_{\Omega}.

Renaming
\overline{w}=\boldsymbol{a}w\boldsymbol{b}^{-1}\in W_{a}
and translating back results in the required formula

\displaystyle f_{0}|_{\Omega}

\displaystyle=g.f_{0}\circ\overline{w}|_{\Omega}.\qed

We can now conclude the proof of axiom (A2).

Proposition 7.9
.

The axiom

(A2)

For all
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{F}
, if
f_{1}(\mathbb{A})\cap f_{2}(\mathbb{A})\neq\emptyset
, then the set
\Omega:=f_{2}^{-1}\circ f_{1}(\mathbb{A})
is a
W_{a}
-convex set and there exists
w\in W_{a}
such that
f_{2}|_{\Omega}=f_{1}\circ w|_{\Omega}
.

holds for
(\mathcal{B},\mathscr{F})
.

Proof.

Let
g,h\in G_{\mathbb{F}}
with
f_{1}=g.f_{0}
and
f_{2}=h.f_{0}
such that
g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap h.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\neq\emptyset
. We define
\Omega:=(h.f_{0})^{-1}(h.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A}))
. We will first show the second part of (A2). To be able to apply Proposition
7.8
, we act with
h^{-1}
on

h.f_{0}(\Omega)=h.f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap g.f_{0}(\mathbb{A}),

to get

f_{0}(\Omega)=f_{0}(\mathbb{A})\cap(h^{-1}g).f_{0}(\mathbb{A}).

We can apply Proposition
7.8
with
h^{-1}g
to get an element
w\in W_{a}
such that
f_{0}|_{\Omega}=(h^{-1}g).f_{0}\circ w|_{\Omega},

and thus
f_{2}|_{\Omega}=h.f_{0}|_{\Omega}=g.f_{0}\circ w|_{\Omega}=f_{1}\circ w|_{\Omega}
.

It remains to show that
\Omega
is
W_{a}
-convex. Elements
a.o\in f_{0}(\Omega)
are exactly those elements which have the special property that

h.a.o=g.w.a.o,

which is equivalent to
a^{-1}h^{-1}gwa\in G_{\mathbb{F}}(O)
by Theorem
4.19
. If
a=\operatorname{Diag}\left(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\right)\in A_{\mathbb{F}}
, then

(h^{-1}gw)_{ij}\frac{a_{j}}{a_{i}}\in O

in matrix entries. Taking the valuation we obtain

(-v)(\chi_{\alpha_{ij}}(a))=(-v)(a_{i}/a_{j})\geq(-v)((h^{-1}gw)_{ij})\in\Lambda

and these are exactly the inequalities that define affine half-apartments

H_{\alpha_{ij},k}^{+}:=\left\{a.o\in\mathbb{A}\colon(-v)(\chi_{\alpha_{ij}}(a))\geq k\right\}

for
k=(-v)((h^{-1}gw)_{ij})
. Since there are only finitely many pairs
(i,j)
,
\Omega
is a finite intersection of half-apartments, i.e.
W_{a}
-convex.
∎
